# Estimation data, filtering and methodology

> Part of the **MOISE agent edition**: an AI-readable restatement of Argov, Barnea, Binyamini, Borenstein, Elkayam & Rozenshtrom (2012), *MOISE: A DSGE Model for the Israeli Economy*, Bank of Israel Discussion Paper 2012.06. Not affiliated with or endorsed by the Bank of Israel. Equations, numbers and notation are transcribed from the paper; explanations are paraphrased. Conventions and verification status: [README](README.md).

4.1–4.3 data, filtering of the observed data and the estimation method; Appendix A (data) and Appendix B (model-consistent filtering). Equations (95)–(109). The data table is also in `data/appendixA_data.csv`.

<!-- Chunk T05. Part 1: Section 4 opening, 4.1-4.3 (PDF pages 40-47, printed 34-41). Part 2: Appendix A and Appendix B (PDF pages 93-100, printed 87-94). -->
<!-- Part 1 -->

# 4 Estimation of the model

**Summary.** The model is estimated on Israeli quarterly data for 1992:Q1–2009:Q4; the first 12 quarters only initialize the Kalman filter. The sample is full of structural transitions and breaks, so the data are filtered in a model-consistent way (4.2), which makes it possible to fit a cyclical, balanced-growth model. The model is log-linearized and estimated by Bayesian methods (4.3); calibration and priors, results and sensitivity analysis follow in 4.4–4.6, which are outside this chunk.

## 4.1 Data

**Summary.** Twenty-four quarterly series are used; most enter as log differences (94.i2), except interest rates, the VAT rate and the current account (entered as a share of GDP). Hours, employment and the domestic national-accounts aggregates are per capita, and every series is seasonally adjusted apart from interest and exchange rates, tax rates and the oil price. Nominal series (inflation rates, exchange-rate changes, nominal wage growth and interest rates) are detrended by subtracting the inflation target. Model labor is per-capita hours only, with no split into hours per worker and number of workers, so a "semi-theoretical" employment equation (95), following Smets and Wouters (2003) and Christoffel et al. (2008), lets employment data inform the hours gap.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 94.i2; section: 4.1; page: 34; pdf_page: 40 -->
**(94.i2) Default transformation of the observables: log difference**

$$
\Delta X_{t} \equiv \log\left(\frac{X_{t}}{X_{t-1}}\right)
$$

Meaning: Most observables enter as the log of the ratio of the current to the previous quarter's value.

### Observable variables as listed in Section 4.1 — page 35, pdf_page 41

The paper prints these in two columns: the first twelve rows below are the left column, the last twelve the right column.

| Series (as printed) | Symbol (as printed) |
|---|---|
| GDP | $\Delta Y_{t}$ |
| Private consumption | $\Delta C_{t}$ |
| Fixed capital investment | $\Delta I_{t}$ |
| Government consumption | $\Delta G_{t}$ |
| Exports | $\Delta X_{t}$ |
| Imports | $\Delta IM_{t}$ |
| GDP deflator | $\Delta P_{Y,t}^{M}$ |
| Export deflator | $\Delta P_{X,t}^{NIS} = \Delta\left(S_{t} P_{X,t}\right)$ |
| Current account | $s_{CA,t} = CA_{t}/P_{Y,t}^{M} Y_{t}$ |
| CPI | $\Delta P_{C,t}$ |
| Inflation target (annualized) | $4 \cdot \bar{\pi}_{t}$ |
| Nominal exchange rate | $\Delta S_{t}$ |
| Nominal hourly wage | $\Delta W_{t}$ |
| Hours worked | $\Delta N_{t}$ |
| Employment | $\Delta EM_{t}$ |
| Bank of Israel key interest rate | $r_{t}^{OB}$ |
| 5-10 year *fwd* real rate | $rr_{t}^{fwd,OB}$ |
| VAT rate | $\tau_{t}^{C}$ |
| G4 nominal interest rate | $r_{t}^{*,OB}$ |
| G4 CPI | $\Delta P_{Y,t}^{*}$ |
| G4 GDP | $\Delta Y_{t}^{*}$ |
| OECD imports | $\Delta WT_{t}^{*}$ |
| 5-10 year *fwd* G4 nominal interest rate | $r_{t}^{*,fwd,OB}$ |
| Price of oil | $\Delta P_{OIL,t}^{*}$ |

<!-- kind: inline; id: 94.i3; section: 4.1; page: 35; pdf_page: 41 -->
**(94.i3) Export deflator in domestic currency (observable definition)**

$$
\Delta P_{X,t}^{NIS} = \Delta\left(S_{t} P_{X,t}\right)
$$

Meaning: The shekel export-price observable is the log change of the exchange rate times the foreign-currency export price.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 94.i4; section: 4.1; page: 35; pdf_page: 41 -->
**(94.i4) Current account ratio (observable definition)**

$$
s_{CA,t} = CA_{t}/P_{Y,t}^{M} Y_{t}
$$

Meaning: The current-account observable is the current account divided by nominal GDP (market-price GDP deflator times real GDP).
Note: printed here with "$=$"; Appendix A (97.i104) prints the same definition with "$\equiv$".

<!-- kind: inline; id: 94.i5; section: 4.1; page: 35; pdf_page: 41 -->
**(94.i5) Annualized inflation target (observable)**

$$
4 \cdot \bar{\pi}_{t}
$$

Meaning: The inflation target is observed in annualized form, four times the quarterly target.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 95; section: 4.1; page: 36; pdf_page: 42 -->
**(95) Employment equation (employment adjusts gradually toward hours worked)**

$$
\begin{aligned}
\widehat{EM}_{t} ={}& \frac{\beta}{1+\beta \chi_{EM}} E_{t}\left[\widehat{EM}_{t+1}\right] + \frac{\chi_{EM}}{1+\beta \chi_{EM}} \widehat{EM}_{t-1} \\
&+ \frac{\left(1-\beta \xi_{EM}\right)\left(1-\xi_{EM}\right)}{\xi_{EM}\left(1+\beta \chi_{EM}\right)}\left(\hat{N}_{t} - \widehat{EM}_{t}\right) + \varepsilon_{t}^{EM}
\end{aligned}
\tag{95}
$$

Meaning: The employment deviation depends on its expected and lagged values and closes the gap to hours worked gradually, at a Calvo-like speed set by $\xi_{EM}$, plus a shock.
Note: the hat on $N$ is printed wide here and in the sentence below (95). It is written $\hat{N}$ under the single-letter rule and is the same variable as $\hat{N}_{t}$ in (99).

Hats denote log deviations from steady state. Employment is assumed to adjust slowly, while hours are flexible. A larger $\xi_{EM}$ means employment reacts less to hours, and $\chi_{EM}$ makes employment persistent. Footnote 20 says $\chi_{EM}$ is the authors' addition, absent from Smets and Wouters (2003) and Christoffel et al. (2008), meant to loosen the link between hours and employment. $\varepsilon_{t}^{EM}$ is not a structural shock and does not feed back into the rest of the model. The equation also serves to forecast employment from predicted hours, and footnote 21 calls it a form of "additive hybrid model".

## 4.2 Filtering the observed data

**Summary.** The sample spans many structural transitions (disinflation, capital-flow and exchange-rate liberalization, shifting pass-through, mass immigration, a changing export mix, a swing from current-account deficits to surpluses, a smaller government-spending share, growing openness), so many real aggregates trend at rates different from output (Figure 2). The model has a single balanced growth path set by productivity growth $g_{z}$; fitting raw data would force its transitory shocks to absorb the uneven trends, and univariate pre-filtering would discard information. Instead, excess trends are estimated jointly with the parameters and shocks ("additive hybrid models", Schorfheide 2011; Canova 2009), with the multivariate Kalman smoother removing only what the model's cyclical dynamics cannot explain. In a typical real-variable observation equation (95.u1) the shared trend splits into technology growth $\hat{g}_{z,t}$ and an AR trend in hours growth $GR_{t}^{N}$ (identified from hours and employment), plus an AR(1) idiosyncratic trend shock $EX_{t}$. Interest rates look non-stationary and are handled like trending variables, the two forward rates getting observation equations with unobserved time-varying term premia; Appendix B lists every observation equation.

**Figure 2: Observable Trending Variables (solid line) and Trends (dashed line).** (page 38, pdf_page 44)

- **Layout:** fifteen panels in a 5 × 3 grid. The x-axis ticks are at 1995, 2000, 2005 and 2010, and each series spans the sample.
  - Row 1: $N$, $EM$, $Y$.
  - Row 2: $C/Y$, $I/Y$, $G/Y$.
  - Row 3: $X/Y$, $IM/Y$, $Y^{*}$.
  - Row 4: $WT^{*}$, $S$, $W/P_{C}$.
  - Row 5: $P_{OIL}^{*}/P_{Y}^{*}$, "$rr^{fwd,OB}$ and $rr^{fwd}$", "$r^{*,fwd,OB}$ and $rr^{*,fwd}$".
- **Lines:** the solid line is the observed trending variable and the dashed line its estimated trend. In the two forward-rate panels the dashed line is the smoothed expected short real rate 5–10 years ahead (see B.4).
- **Figure note:** the trends come from the stochastic growth rate $g_{z}$ and the additive components of Section 4.2, and interest rates are annualized.
- **Scales:** each panel has its own y-axis scale; no units are stated.
- **Approximate reading:** in both forward-rate panels the observed rate lies above the smoothed expected real rate over the whole sample.

<!-- kind: display; id: 95.u1; section: 4.2; page: 39; pdf_page: 45 -->
**(95.u1) Typical observation equation for a real variable**

$$
\Delta X_{t} = \hat{x}_{t} - \hat{x}_{t-1} + J^{g} \cdot \left(\hat{g}_{z,t} + g_{z}\right) + J^{N} \cdot GR_{t}^{N} + EX_{t}
$$

Meaning: Observed growth equals the change in the model counterpart plus, via 0/1 selection indicators, technology growth and hours-trend growth, plus an AR(1) idiosyncratic trend shock $EX_{t}$.
Note: the hat on $g$ is printed wide, here and in the text below; it is written $\hat{g}_{z,t}$. This generic form has $\left(\hat{g}_{z,t} + g_{z}\right)$ with no $-1$, whereas (101) and (104) have $\hat{g}_{z,t} + \left(g_{z}-1\right)$. Transcribed as printed (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T05.md)).

## 4.3 Methodology

**Summary.** Estimation is full-information, likelihood-based and Bayesian (An and Schorfheide 2007), the standard approach for medium-scale policy DSGE models, and is run in Dynare 4 under Matlab. The posterior of model $M_{i}$'s parameters $\theta_{i}$ given data $y$ is likelihood (from the Kalman filter) times prior over the marginal data density (96); since that denominator does not involve $\theta_{i}$, the posterior is proportional to the kernel $K$ (97). With no analytical form available, the posterior mode is found by numerical optimization, and random-walk Metropolis–Hastings draws then give the posterior mean and selected intervals. Models are compared by Bayes factors, which under equal prior model probabilities equal the ratio of marginal data densities, each the kernel integrated over the parameters (97.u1).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 96; section: 4.3; page: 40; pdf_page: 46 -->
**(96) Posterior distribution of the parameters (Bayes' rule)**

$$
p\left(\theta_{i} \mid y, M_{i}\right) = \frac{p\left(y \mid \theta_{i}, M_{i}\right) p\left(\theta_{i} \mid M_{i}\right)}{p\left(y \mid M_{i}\right)}
\tag{96}
$$

Meaning: The posterior of model i's parameters given the data equals the likelihood times the prior, divided by the marginal density of the data under model i.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 96.i1; section: 4.3; page: 40; pdf_page: 46 -->
**(96.i1) Likelihood function**

$$
p\left(y \mid \theta_{i}, M_{i}\right)
$$

Meaning: The likelihood of the data given model i and its parameters; the paper evaluates it with the Kalman filter.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 96.i2; section: 4.3; page: 40; pdf_page: 46 -->
**(96.i2) Prior distribution**

$$
p\left(\theta_{i} \mid M_{i}\right)
$$

Meaning: The prior on model i's parameters, expressing the researcher's beliefs before seeing the data.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 97; section: 4.3; page: 40; pdf_page: 46 -->
**(97) Posterior kernel**

$$
p\left(\theta_{i} \mid y, M_{i}\right) \propto p\left(y \mid \theta_{i}, M_{i}\right) p\left(\theta_{i} \mid M_{i}\right) = K\left(\theta_{i} \mid y, M_{i}\right)
\tag{97}
$$

Meaning: Without the parameter-free denominator, the posterior is proportional to likelihood times prior, a product denoted by the kernel $K$.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i1; section: 4.3; page: 40; pdf_page: 46 -->
**(97.i1) Posterior probability of model i (what the Bayes factor compares)**

$$
p\left(M_{i} \mid y\right)
$$

Meaning: Probability of model i given the data. The text says the Bayes factor $BF_{ij}$ compares these across models and calls them the models' "marginal likelihoods".

<!-- kind: display; id: 97.u1; section: 4.3; page: 41; pdf_page: 47 -->
**(97.u1) Bayes factor between models i and j**

$$
BF_{ij} = \frac{p\left(M_{i} \mid y\right)}{p\left(M_{j} \mid y\right)} = \frac{p\left(y \mid M_{i}\right)}{p\left(y \mid M_{j}\right)} = \frac{\int p\left(y \mid \theta_{i}, M_{i}\right) p\left(\theta_{i} \mid M_{i}\right) d\theta_{i}}{\int p\left(y \mid \theta_{j}, M_{j}\right) p\left(\theta_{j} \mid M_{j}\right) d\theta_{j}} = \frac{\int K\left(\theta_{i} \mid y, M_{i}\right) d\theta_{i}}{\int K\left(\theta_{j} \mid y, M_{j}\right) d\theta_{j}}
$$

Meaning: The Bayes factor is the ratio of posterior model probabilities. With equal prior model probabilities it equals the ratio of marginal data densities, which are the integrated kernels.

The text explains each equality in turn: the second applies Bayes' rule under equal prior probabilities of the two models, the third integrates out each model's parameters, and the last uses the kernel defined in (97).

# Appendices

## Appendix A The data

**Summary.** The estimation data are 24 quarterly series for 1992:Q1–2009:Q4. Unless a series is said to be treated differently, it enters as a one-quarter log difference (97.i101). Everything except interest and exchange rates, tax rates and the oil price is seasonally adjusted, and per-capita series are divided by the working-age population. The table below lists the series by the paper's six categories; defining expressions printed inside the list follow it as separate math blocks.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i101; section: A; page: 87; pdf_page: 93 -->
**(97.i101) Default transformation of the observed series: log difference**

$$
\Delta X_{t} \equiv \log\left(x_{t}/x_{t-1}\right)
$$

Meaning: Unless stated otherwise, each observable is the log of the ratio of its current to its previous quarterly value.
Note: printed with upper-case $X_{t}$ on the left and lower-case $x_{t}$, $x_{t-1}$ inside the log. The Section 4.1 version (94.i2) is upper case throughout and uses a stacked fraction. Transcribed as printed (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T05.md)).

It states them once, after the list of foreign series. The OECD source is printed as "Source OECD database" (no colon) for OECD imports.

### Real National Accounts data

**Summary.** These are six national-accounts aggregates, all per capita and at constant prices. Volatile, low-value-added subcomponents unrelated to the business cycle are left out (ships and aircraft, diamonds, start-ups, defence imports). Data on the SNA93 standard start only in 1995:Q1, so earlier quarters follow the previous standard. The CBS supplies all six series already seasonally adjusted.

### Prices and Inflation

**Summary.** Fruit and vegetables are removed from the CPI. Housing is also removed for 1992–2006, when most rental contracts were in dollars. Model CPI inflation ($\hat{\pi}_{C,t}$) is measured before indirect taxes, so VAT-rate changes are subtracted from observed CPI inflation, with a time-varying weight because rents carry no VAT (footnote 51). The GDP-deflator observation equation includes a measurement error (footnote 52). For these nominal price series, the inflation target is subtracted first and seasonal adjustment is done afterwards.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i102; section: A; page: 88; pdf_page: 94 -->
**(97.i102) Export price in domestic currency (observable definition)**

$$
\Delta P_{X,t}^{NIS} = \Delta\left(S_{t} P_{X,t}\right)
$$

Meaning: The shekel export-price observable is the log change of the exchange rate times the foreign-currency export price.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i103; section: A; page: 88; pdf_page: 94 -->
**(97.i103) Annualized inflation target (observable)**

$$
4 \cdot \bar{\pi}_{t}
$$

Meaning: The inflation-target observable is four times the quarterly target (annualized).

### Interest rates and the exchange rate

**Summary.** The policy rate is the Bank of Israel's annualized key rate, and the forward real rate comes from indexed government bond yields. The exchange rate is a four-currency effective rate (a rise is a depreciation), with weights from regressing a broader effective rate on those four currencies. None of these series is seasonally adjusted, and the inflation target is subtracted from the nominal rate and from the exchange-rate change.

### Labor market data

**Summary.** The hourly wage is the total wage bill over total hours, net of the inflation target. Hours and employment are both per working-age person. The data come from the National Insurance Institute and the CBS, and the CBS does the seasonal adjustment.

### Foreign data

**Summary.** The foreign variables are G4 aggregates (policy rate, CPI, GDP), OECD import volumes, and a G4 5–10 year forward nominal rate from non-indexed bonds. The weights are the exchange-rate weights: USA 49%, Euro area 32%, Britain 13%, Japan 6%.

### Miscellaneous data

**Summary.** This group contains the VAT rate (Ministry of Finance) and the current-account surplus as a share of nominal GDP (CBS). It also contains the Brent crude price per barrel in effective-exchange-rate terms (Bloomberg).

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i104; section: A; page: 90; pdf_page: 96 -->
**(97.i104) Current account surplus ratio (observable definition)**

$$
s_{CA,t} \equiv CA_{t}/P_{Y,t}^{M} Y_{t}
$$

Meaning: The current-account observable is the current-account surplus divided by nominal GDP (market-price deflator times real output).
Note: printed with "$\equiv$" here and with "$=$" in Section 4.1 (94.i4).

## Appendix B Model-consistent filtering

**Summary.** In the model, all real variables grow at one common rate, the labor-augmenting technology growth $\hat{g}_{z,t}$ (equation (18)). In the data, different aggregates trend at different speeds. Each observable therefore gets an explicitly modelled "excess trend", estimated together with the model; this appendix gives those trend processes and the observation equations built on them.

### B.1 Detrending employment variables

**Summary.** One secular growth component for per-capita hours, $GR_{t}^{N}$, follows an AR(1) process. It removes the excess trend from hours and employment and, through output, from the real domestic variables. Footnote 53 says the long-run growth of per-capita hours must strictly be zero. The AR means are treated as long-run growth rates only within the sample. The three observation equations below tie the observed growth rates of hours, employment and output per capita to model deviations from steady state.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 98; section: B.1; page: 91; pdf_page: 97 -->
**(98) Secular growth component of hours worked (AR(1))**

$$
GR_{t}^{N} = \left(1-\rho_{GR}^{N}\right) g_{\Delta N} + \rho_{GR}^{N} GR_{t-1}^{N} + \eta_{GR,t}^{N}
\tag{98}
$$

Meaning: Trend growth of per-capita hours is AR(1) around the long-run rate $g_{\Delta N}$, with estimated persistence $\rho_{GR}^{N}$ and an i.i.d. innovation $\eta_{GR,t}^{N}$.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 98.i1; section: B.1; page: 91; pdf_page: 97 -->
**(98.i1) Footnote 54: log deviation of stationarized output**

$$
\hat{y}_{t} = y_{t} - y
$$

Meaning: Hatted output is the deviation of stationarized log output from its steady-state value $y$.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 98.i2; section: B.1; page: 91; pdf_page: 97 -->
**(98.i2) Footnote 54: stationarized log output**

$$
y_{t} = \log\left(Y_{t}/z_{t}\right)
$$

Meaning: Real domestic variables are made stationary by dividing by the permanent technology level $z_{t}$; for output this gives the log of $Y_{t}/z_{t}$.
Note: the footnote calls $y$ "the steady state value of $Y_{t}/z_{t}$". Because $y_{t}$ is a log, $y$ should be the steady state of $y_{t}$, i.e. of the log ratio. The wording is kept as printed (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T05.md)).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 99; section: B.1; page: 91; pdf_page: 97 -->
**(99) Observation equation: hours worked**

$$
\Delta N_{t}^{OB} = \hat{N}_{t} - \hat{N}_{t-1} + GR_{t}^{N}
\tag{99}
$$

Meaning: Observed growth of per-capita hours equals the change in the hours deviation plus the secular hours-growth component.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 100; section: B.1; page: 92; pdf_page: 98 -->
**(100) Observation equation: employment**

$$
\Delta EM_{t}^{OB} = \widehat{EM}_{t} - \widehat{EM}_{t-1} + GR_{t}^{N}
\tag{100}
$$

Meaning: Observed employment-rate growth equals the change in the employment deviation plus the same secular hours-growth component.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 101; section: B.1; page: 92; pdf_page: 98 -->
**(101) Observation equation: per capita output (GDP)**

$$
\Delta Y_{t}^{OB} = \hat{y}_{t} - \hat{y}_{t-1} + \hat{g}_{z,t} + \left(g_{z}-1\right) + GR_{t}^{N}
\tag{101}
$$

Meaning: Observed per-capita GDP growth equals the change in stationarized output, plus technology growth ($\hat{g}_{z,t}$ plus the net steady-state rate $g_{z}-1$), plus secular hours growth.

The superscript $OB$ marks the observed variables, which still contain the excess trends, as distinct from their model counterparts.

### B.2 Detrending aggregate quantities of goods

**Summary.** Private consumption, investment, exports, imports, foreign GDP and world trade each have an AR(1) excess trend $EX_{t}^{J}$. Its mean is the component's long-run growth less technology growth and, for domestic components only, less hours growth; an indicator $j^{H}$ switches the hours term on. Government consumption has no AR process of its own: its excess trend follows from a share-weighted condition that keeps the long-run resource constraint satisfied. One observation equation, (104), then covers all seven real components. Footnote 55 says foreign excess growth is measured relative to $g_{z}$, and domestic excess growth relative to the long-run growth of observed GDP.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 101.i1; section: B.2; page: 92; pdf_page: 98 -->
**(101.i1) Components that have an AR excess-trend process**

$$
J \in \left\{C, I, X, IM, Y^{*}, WT^{*}\right\}
$$

Meaning: The AR excess-trend process (102) is specified for consumption, investment, exports, imports, foreign GDP and world trade (not government consumption).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 102; section: B.2; page: 92; pdf_page: 98 -->
**(102) Excess trend of component J (AR(1))**

$$
EX_{t}^{J} = \left(1-\rho_{EX}^{J}\right)\left(g_{\Delta J} - g_{z} - j^{H}\left(J\right) \cdot g_{\Delta N}\right) + \rho_{EX}^{J} EX_{t-1}^{J} + \eta_{EX,t}^{J}
\tag{102}
$$

Meaning: Each component's excess trend is AR(1) around its long-run excess growth, with estimated persistence $\rho_{EX}^{J}$ and an i.i.d. innovation $\eta_{EX,t}^{J}$.

<!-- kind: display; id: 102.u1; section: B.2; page: 92; pdf_page: 98 -->
**(102.u1) Domestic-component indicator function**

$$
j^{H}\left(J\right) = \left\{ \begin{array}{lll} 1 & if & J \in \left\{C, I, X, IM\right\} \\ 0 & if & J \in \left\{Y^{*}, WT^{*}\right\} \end{array} \right.
$$

Meaning: The indicator is one for domestic components, which carry the secular hours growth, and zero for foreign GDP and world trade.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 102.i1; section: B.2; page: 92; pdf_page: 98 -->
**(102.i1) Long-run (idiosyncratic) excess growth of component J**

$$
g_{\Delta J} - g_{z} - j^{H}\left(J\right) \cdot g_{\Delta N}
$$

Meaning: The assumed long-run growth $g_{\Delta J}$ of component J, less technology growth and, for domestic components, less hours growth.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 102.i2; section: B.2; page: 92; pdf_page: 98 -->
**(102.i2) Footnote 55: long-run growth rate of observed GDP**

$$
g_{z} + g_{\Delta N} - 1
$$

Meaning: Domestic components' excess growth is measured against this, the long-run growth of observed GDP; foreign variables' excess growth is measured against $g_{z}$.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 103; section: B.2; page: 93; pdf_page: 99 -->
**(103) Long-run resource-constraint condition on excess trends (determines government consumption)**

$$
s_{C} EX_{t}^{C} + s_{I} EX_{t}^{I} + s_{G} EX_{t}^{G} + s_{X} EX_{t}^{X} - s_{IM} EX_{t}^{IM} = 0
\tag{103}
$$

Meaning: The GDP-share-weighted excess trends of the expenditure components, with imports subtracted, sum to zero; this determines government consumption's excess trend $EX_{t}^{G}$.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 103.i1; section: B.2; page: 93; pdf_page: 99 -->
**(103.i1) Real components covered by the observation equation (104)**

$$
J \in \left\{C, I, G, X, IM, Y^{*}, WT^{*}\right\}
$$

Meaning: Observation equation (104) applies to all seven real components, including government consumption.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 103.i2; section: B.2; page: 93; pdf_page: 99 -->
**(103.i2) Stationarized component J (its log deviation from steady state is denoted $\hat{\jmath}_{t}$)**

$$
J_{t}/z_{t}
$$

Meaning: Each real component is scaled by technology $z_{t}$; $\hat{\jmath}_{t}$ is the log deviation of this ratio from its steady state.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 104; section: B.2; page: 93; pdf_page: 99 -->
**(104) Observation equation: real component J**

$$
\Delta J_{t}^{OB} = \hat{\jmath}_{t} - \hat{\jmath}_{t-1} + \hat{g}_{z,t} + \left(g_{z}-1\right) + j^{H} \cdot GR_{t}^{N} + EX_{t}^{J}
\tag{104}
$$

Meaning: Observed growth of component J equals the change in its stationarized deviation, plus technology growth, secular hours growth (domestic components only) and its own excess trend.
Note: $j^{H}$ is printed here without its argument $\left(J\right)$, unlike (102) and 102.u1.

### B.3 Detrending relative prices

**Summary.** Three relative prices also get model-consistent excess trends: the real exchange rate, the real hourly wage and the oil price relative to foreign prices. The exchange-rate and wage equations subtract the inflation-target deviation $\hat{\bar{\pi}}_{t}$, mirroring the target subtraction applied to the nominal data (Section 4.1). $EX_{t}^{S}$ and $EX_{t}^{W}$ are i.i.d. rather than AR, which avoids long-lived gaps between observed and model exchange-rate changes and treats the wage term as noise. The oil excess trend $EX_{t}^{P_{OIL}^{*}}$ is AR(1), zero in steady state, with persistence $\rho_{EX}^{P_{OIL}^{*}}$ (as in (102)), and can represent permanent relative-price shifts or simply observation error.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 105; section: B.3; page: 93; pdf_page: 99 -->
**(105) Observation equation: nominal exchange rate (depreciation)**

$$
\Delta S_{t}^{OB} = \hat{s}_{t} - \hat{s}_{t-1} + \left(\hat{\pi}_{Y,t} + \bar{\Pi} - 1\right) - \left(\hat{\pi}_{Y,t}^{*} + \bar{\Pi}^{*} - 1\right) - \hat{\bar{\pi}}_{t} + EX_{t}^{S}
\tag{105}
$$

Meaning: Observed depreciation equals the change in the real exchange rate, plus domestic minus foreign inflation (each deviation plus steady state), minus the target deviation, plus an i.i.d. term.
Note: the accent on $\pi$ is printed as a wide hat over a bar (here, in (106) and in (109)). It is written $\hat{\bar{\pi}}$, the notation EXAMPLE.md uses for the same variable.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 106; section: B.3; page: 93; pdf_page: 99 -->
**(106) Observation equation: nominal hourly wage**

$$
\Delta W_{t}^{OB} = \hat{w}_{t} + \hat{w}_{t-1} + \left(\hat{\pi}_{C,t} + \bar{\Pi} - 1\right) + \left(\hat{g}_{z,t} + g_{z} - 1\right) - \hat{\bar{\pi}}_{t} + EX_{t}^{W}
\tag{106}
$$

Meaning: Observed nominal wage growth links to the scaled real-wage terms, CPI inflation (deviation plus steady state) and technology growth, less the target deviation, plus i.i.d. noise.
Note: printed "$+\hat{w}_{t-1}$". Equations (99)–(105) and (107) all use the change $\hat{x}_{t}-\hat{x}_{t-1}$, and the text says (106) ties wage growth to the real-wage deviation, so "$-$" is probably intended. Transcribed as printed (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T05.md)).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 107; section: B.3; page: 93; pdf_page: 99 -->
**(107) Observation equation: oil price (foreign currency)**

$$
\Delta P_{OIL,t}^{*,OB} = \hat{p}_{OIL,t}^{*} - \hat{p}_{OIL,t-1}^{*} + \Delta P_{Y,t}^{*} + EX_{t}^{P_{OIL}^{*}}
\tag{107}
$$

Meaning: Observed quarterly oil-price growth equals the change in the relative oil price, plus foreign price inflation, plus an AR(1) excess-trend term.
Note: the foreign-inflation term is written $\Delta P_{Y,t}^{*}$, the symbol of the observed G4 CPI series, not a model term such as the $\hat{\pi}_{Y,t}^{*} + \bar{\Pi}^{*} - 1$ used in (105). Transcribed as printed.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 107.i1; section: B.3; page: 93; pdf_page: 99 -->
**(107.i1) Real exchange rate (level definition)**

$$
s_{t} = S_{t} \cdot P_{Y,t}^{*}/P_{Y,t}
$$

Meaning: The real exchange rate is the nominal rate times the foreign price level, divided by the domestic output price. $\hat{s}_{t}$ is its deviation from steady state.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 107.i2; section: B.3; page: 94; pdf_page: 100 -->
**(107.i2) Scaled real wage**

$$
w_{t} = \frac{W_{t}}{P_{C,t} z_{t}}
$$

Meaning: The nominal hourly wage, deflated by the CPI and scaled by technology. $\hat{w}_{t}$ is its log deviation from steady state.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 107.i3; section: B.3; page: 94; pdf_page: 100 -->
**(107.i3) Relative price of oil**

$$
p_{OIL.t}^{*} = P_{OIL,t}^{*}/P_{Y,t}^{*}
$$

Meaning: The relative oil price is the foreign-currency oil price divided by the foreign price level. $\hat{p}_{OIL,t}^{*}$ is its deviation from steady state.
Note: the left-hand subscript is printed "$OIL.t$", with a period, both here and in the $\hat{p}_{OIL.t}^{*}$ just before it; everywhere else it is "$OIL,t$". It is probably a typo for a comma. Transcribed as printed (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T05.md)).

### B.4 Smoothing of forward interest rates

**Summary.** The observed 5–10 year forward rates are tied to the short rates the model expects 5–10 years ahead; the domestic forward rate is real, the foreign one nominal, and the bracketed terms are multiplied by 4 because the data are annualized. Each equation adds a constant term premium ($tp$, $tp^{*}$, both estimated) to account for yield curves that normally slope upward, plus a serially correlated observation error that may also capture time variation in the premium. For the foreign rate, agents are assumed to expect average foreign inflation 5–10 years ahead to sit at its target ($\hat{\bar{\pi}}_{t}^{*}$). The appendix ends at the top of PDF p.101, noting that Figure 2 plots the observed forward rates against the smoothed expected real rates $\widehat{rr}_{t}^{fwd}$ and $\widehat{rr}_{t}^{*,fwd}$.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 108; section: B.4; page: 94; pdf_page: 100 -->
**(108) Observation equation: domestic 5-10 year forward real interest rate**

$$
rr_{t}^{fwd,OB} = 4 \cdot \left(\frac{g_{z}}{\beta} - 1 + \widehat{rr}_{t}^{fwd}\right) + tp + \varepsilon_{t}^{fwd,OB}
\tag{108}
$$

Meaning: The observed annualized forward real rate is four times the steady-state net real rate plus the expected real-rate deviation, plus a constant term premium and an observation error.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 109; section: B.4; page: 94; pdf_page: 100 -->
**(109) Observation equation: foreign 5-10 year forward nominal interest rate**

$$
r_{t}^{*,fwd,OB} = 4 \cdot \left(\frac{g_{z}}{\beta} \bar{\Pi}^{*} - 1 + \widehat{rr}_{t}^{*,fwd} + \hat{\bar{\pi}}_{t}^{*}\right) + tp^{*} + \varepsilon_{t}^{*,fwd,OB}
\tag{109}
$$

Meaning: The observed annualized foreign forward nominal rate is four times the steady-state net nominal rate plus expected real-rate and target-inflation deviations, plus a term premium and an error.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i1; section: B.4; page: 94; pdf_page: 100 -->
**(109.i1) Steady-state (gross) real interest rate**

$$
g_{z}/\beta
$$

Meaning: Productivity growth divided by the discount factor gives the steady-state gross real interest rate used in (108) and (109).

# Calibration, shocks, priors and estimation results

> Part of the **MOISE agent edition**: an AI-readable restatement of Argov, Barnea, Binyamini, Borenstein, Elkayam & Rozenshtrom (2012), *MOISE: A DSGE Model for the Israeli Economy*, Bank of Israel Discussion Paper 2012.06. Not affiliated with or endorsed by the Bank of Israel. Equations, numbers and notation are transcribed from the paper; explanations are paraphrased. Conventions and verification status: [README](README.md).

4.4–4.6: calibrated parameters (Table 1), structural shocks, priors and posteriors (Tables 2–4), sensitivity to the interest-rate rule (Table 5). Every table is also a CSV in `data/`.

<!-- Chunk T06: 4.4 Calibrated parameters, shocks and prior distributions (4.4.1-4.4.3); 4.5 Estimation results; 4.6 Sensitivity analysis. PDF pages 47-67 (printed 41-61). Starts at the 4.4 heading (PDF p.47, below the Bayes-factor display of 4.3); stops before "5 Model evaluation" (PDF p.68). -->

*Printed pp. 41–61 (PDF pp. 47–67).* Machine-readable tables: `data/table1_calibrated.csv`, `table2_structural.csv`, `table3_observation_and_shocks.csv`, `table4_world.csv`, `table5_sensitivity.csv`, `shocks.csv`. All numbers are kept as printed text. In the CSVs, `row_type` = `group_label` marks the italic group-heading rows of Tables 2–5 (kept as their own rows); `data` marks parameter rows; `summary` marks the marginal-likelihood row of Table 5.

## 4.4 Calibrated parameters, shocks and prior distributions

**Summary.** Two kinds of parameters are distinguished. Those that pin down the steady state are calibrated so that the steady state reproduces assumed long-run great ratios (GDP shares), production input weights, or (imbalanced) growth rates. Those that affect only the dynamics are, in general, estimated. Subsection 4.4.1 covers calibration, 4.4.2 lists the structural shocks used in estimation, and 4.4.3 explains how the priors of the estimated parameters were chosen.

### 4.4.1 Calibrated parameters

**Summary.** Table 1 reports the calibrated values. They are chosen so that steady-state ratios match long-horizon data averages or the values the economy is expected to converge to.

- **Great ratios (steady state):** private consumption 55% of GDP, fixed investment 21%, inventory investment ($\Delta inv$) 1%, government consumption ($s_{G}$) 26%, exports 39%, imports 42%. Foreign transfers to GDP ($s_{FTR}$) are set at 3% to finance the steady-state trade deficit.
- **Inflation objective ($\bar{\Pi}$):** 2% per year, the middle of the post-disinflation target range. Table 1 lists the quarterly gross value 1.005.
- **Labor-productivity growth ($g_{z}$):** 1.0% per year, roughly the sample average. Table 1 lists the quarterly gross value 1.0025.
- **Capital weight ($\alpha$):** chosen so that the wage bill is 60% of GDP. Footnote 22 notes that $(1-\alpha)=0.67$ nonetheless, because the cost of working capital is not counted in the wage bill and because of the adjustment to market prices.
- **Depreciation ($\delta$):** 2% per quarter, roughly the long-run average gap between gross and net returns on capital.
- **Discount factor ($\beta$):** set to deliver a 2.9% steady-state real interest rate.
- **Common practice in the literature:**
  - The inverse labor-supply elasticity ($\zeta$) is 2.0.
  - Steady-state markups are 30% for wages, domestic prices and import prices ($\varphi^{W}$, $\varphi^{H}$, $\varphi^{*}$ = 1.3). The export markup ($\varphi^{X}$) is a smaller 10%, because exporters' input price ($P_{t}^{DX}$) already includes the domestic and import markups.
- **Elasticities of substitution (domestic vs. imported intermediates):**
  - Consumption, investment and exports ($\mu^{C}$, $\mu^{I}$, $\mu^{X}$): 1.1. This is below common literature values (footnote 23: a 1.5 prior in Christoffel et al. 2008; a 5.0 calibration in Adolfson et al. 2007) but above the 0.4 estimate for Israel (Friedman and Lavi 2007).
  - Government ($\mu^{G}$): 0.2, because government spending is mostly public-sector wages, which cannot be substituted.
  - Foreign elasticity across import origins ($\mu^{*}$): 1.5, a common value.
- **Home-bias parameters ($\nu^{C}$, $\nu^{I}$, $\nu^{G}$, $\nu^{X}$):** set to give steady-state import intensities of 31% (consumption), 42% (investment), 5% (government) and 32% (exports). Footnote 24 stresses the large import share in exports and notes that several reference models treat exports as pure value added.
- **Taxes, set to finance steady-state government consumption:**
  - Consumption tax $\tau^{C}$ = 16%, levied on 78% of the basket ($w_{\tau^{C}}$). Footnote 25: housing services and fresh fruit and vegetables, 22% of the basket, carry no VAT.
  - Labor-income tax $\tau^{N}$ = 28%.
  - Household payroll tax $\tau^{W_{h}}$ = 9% and firm payroll tax $\tau^{W_{f}}$ = 7%.
  - Capital-income tax $\tau^{K}$ = 50%, far above actual profit or capital-gains rates, to fine-tune the steady-state investment/GDP ratio.
  - Government transfers to GDP ($s_{TR}$) are set to balance the steady-state budget, which does not affect the linearized model.
- **Export demand:** the export competitors' relative price ($p_{X}^{C}$) and relative foreign technology ($\tilde{z}$) are normalized to 1.0. Israel's steady-state weight in world trade ($\nu^{*}$) is 0.5%. None of the three affects the linearized model.
- **Working capital:** the weight of wage-bill loans ($\nu^{F}$) is 0.2, so that the working-capital channel operates.
- Values that appear only in the text (not in Table 1) are collected in `data/calibration_in_text.csv`.
- **Dynamic parameters fixed at zero (inline block 97.i3):** wages are indexed to steady-state rather than actual productivity growth, and final-goods production has no import-intensity adjustment costs. The utilization cost $\gamma_{u,2}$ is set to 10,000, so capital utilization does not vary.
- **Long-run growth rates (non-structural, annual):**
  - Per-capita hours: 0.7%. With productivity growth this gives 1.7% per-capita GDP growth; both are roughly sample averages.
  - Foreign GDP $g_{\Delta Y^{*}}$ 2.2%, world trade $g_{\Delta WT^{*}}$ 5.3%, per-capita consumption $g_{\Delta C}$ 1.7%, investment $g_{\Delta I}$ 1.7%, exports $g_{\Delta X}$ 4.9%, imports $g_{\Delta IM}$ 2.9%. These rest on sample averages plus a judgment that the sample's excess consumption and investment growth is transitory.
  - Implied per-capita government consumption growth $g_{\Delta G}$: −1% per year over the sample (printed "(-1%)").

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i2; section: 4.4.1; page: 42; pdf_page: 48 -->
**(97.i2) Labor share implied by the capital weight (footnote 22)**

$$
\left(1-\alpha\right) = 0.67
$$

Meaning: Even with a 60% wage-bill share, one minus the capital weight equals 0.67, owing to unmeasured working-capital cost and the market-price adjustment.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i3; section: 4.4.1; page: 43; pdf_page: 49 -->
**(97.i3) Dynamic parameters fixed at zero: wage indexation to actual productivity growth, final-goods import-intensity adjustment costs**

$$
\chi_{W,g_{z}} = \gamma_{IM^{C}} = \gamma_{IM^{I}} = \gamma_{IM^{G}} = \gamma_{IM^{X}} = 0
$$

Meaning: Wage indexation to actual productivity growth and the import-intensity adjustment costs of the four final-goods sectors are all switched off.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i4; section: 4.4.1; page: 43; pdf_page: 49 -->
**(97.i4) Utilization-cost parameter and its calibrated value (printed in words: "setting the cost of variation ($\gamma_{u,2}$) to $10,000$")**

$$
\gamma_{u,2}
$$

Meaning: The capital-utilization cost parameter, set to 10,000 (printed with a thousands comma) so that capital utilization effectively cannot vary.

#### Table 1: Calibrated Parameters (printed p. 44, pdf_page 50)

The table is printed as two side-by-side panels (each with Parameter, symbol and Value columns). The CSV keeps one record per entry, with `side` = left/right and `printed_row` = the line within its panel. The left panel has 17 lines; the right has 16 (line 17 is blank).

| Parameter | Symbol | Value | Parameter | Symbol | Value |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Discount factor | $\beta$ | 0.995 | Wage markup | $\varphi^{W}$ | 1.3 |
| Inverse of labor EoS | $\zeta$ | 2.0 | Dom. price markup | $\varphi^{H}$ | 1.3 |
| Capital share in prod. | $\alpha$ | 0.33 | Imp. price markup | $\varphi^{*}$ | 1.3 |
| LR productivity growth | $g_{z}$ | 1.0025 | Exp. price markup | $\varphi^{X}$ | 1.1 |
| Depreciation rate | $\delta$ | 0.02 | Home bias - C | $\nu^{C}$ | 0.65 |
| EoS in consumption | $\mu^{C}$ | 1.1 | Home bias - I | $\nu^{I}$ | 0.60 |
| EoS in investment | $\mu^{I}$ | 1.1 | Home bias - G | $\nu^{G}$ | 0.95 |
| EoS in government | $\mu^{G}$ | 0.2 | Home bias - X | $\nu^{X}$ | 0.68 |
| EoS in exports | $\mu^{X}$ | 1.1 | Gov. to GDP | $s_{G}$ | 0.26 |
| Foreign EoS | $\mu^{*}$ | 1.5 | Consumption tax | $\tau^{C}$ | 0.16 |
| X's competitors price | $p_{X}^{C}$ | 1.0 | Capital tax | $\tau^{K}$ | 0.50 |
| Relative technology | $\tilde{z}$ | 1.0 | Labor income tax | $\tau^{N}$ | 0.28 |
| X's weight in $IM^{*}$ | $\nu^{*}$ | 0.005 | Payroll tax - $h$ | $\tau^{W_{h}}$ | 0.09 |
| Working capital weight | $\nu^{F}$ | 0.2 | Payroll tax - $f$ | $\tau^{W_{f}}$ | 0.07 |
| Foreign transfers to GDP | $s_{FTR}$ | 0.03 | Gov. transfers to GDP | $s_{TR}$ | 0.15 |
| LR inflation rate | $\bar{\Pi}$ | 1.005 | $\Delta$ Inventories in GDP | $\Delta inv$ | 0.01 |
| Share of taxed goods | $w_{\tau^{C}}$ | 0.78 | | | |

Notes on Table 1:
- $g_{z}$ = 1.0025 and $\bar{\Pi}$ = 1.005 are quarterly gross rates (≈1.0% and 2% per year in the text).
- $\nu^{*}$ = 0.005 is the 0.5% weight of Israel's exports in world trade described in the text.
- The text does not give a numerical $s_{TR}$; the table's 0.15 is the value chosen to balance the budget.

### 4.4.2 Structural shocks

**Summary.** The estimation uses 24 observed series. It involves 25 structural shocks, plus the 14 observation-equation shocks of Appendix B. All structural shocks are AR(1) (footnote 26 refers to footnote 4: in general, log-AR(1)), except the interest-rate (Taylor-rule) shock, which is i.i.d. For each shock the paper lists, in parentheses, the shock symbol, its AR coefficient and its i.i.d. innovation. Four entries list only an innovation: the interest rate, the inflation target, the VAT rate and the world LR rate.

Several shocks are excluded or restricted:
- The labor-supply shock $\varepsilon_{t}^{N}$ is dropped because it is observationally equivalent to the wage-markup shock $\varphi_{t}^{W}$.
- The home-bias variables of all final-good sectors ($\nu_{t}^{C}$, $\nu_{t}^{G}$, $\nu_{t}^{I}$, $\nu_{t}^{X}$) share one innovation $\eta_{t}^{\nu}$ and one AR coefficient $\rho_{\nu}$. This cuts the number of under-identified free parameters, and $\eta_{t}^{\nu}$ acts as a general negative import-demand shock.
- The alternative import-demand shock $\varepsilon_{t}^{IM}$ is therefore not used. It would move final-goods prices directly through the import-intensity adjustment-cost term, which the authors find hard to interpret. Footnote 27: unlike adjustment costs, the effect of $\nu_{t}$ drops out of the log-linearized final-goods price equations (e.g. (45)) when domestic and imported intermediate prices are equal in steady state.
- The export competitors' relative foreign price $p_{X,t}^{c,*}$ is held constant (inline block 97.i5).
- Neither variation in competitors' relative export prices nor the export-demand shock $\varepsilon_{t}^{X}$ is used: in the linearized model (fn. 28) both are observationally equivalent to the time-varying export share $\nu_{t}^{*}$. Footnote 29 adds that competitors' prices are not used because the data cannot separate them from foreign prices in general.
- Owing to data limitations, the social-security tax rates ($\tau_{t}^{W_{h}}$ and $\tau^{W_{f}}$), the capital-income tax ($\tau_{t}^{K}$) and the dividend tax ($\tau_{t}^{D}$) are held constant.
- Estimation with the direct income-tax rate $\tau_{t}^{N}$ gave unsatisfactory results, probably because of its strong downward trend over the sample.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i5; section: 4.4.2; page: 46; pdf_page: 52 -->
**(97.i5) Relative foreign price of export competitors (assumed not to vary)**

$$
p_{X,t}^{c,*} = P_{X,t}^{c,*}/P_{t}^{*}
$$

Meaning: The competitors' export price relative to the foreign price level; the estimation assumes it stays constant.

Note: Section 3.6 (PDF p.40, outside this chunk) prints the same assumption as $p_{X,t}^{c,*} \equiv P_{X,t}^{c,*}/P_{Y,t}^{*} = 1$, with $P_{Y,t}^{*}$ in the denominator. Here the denominator is printed $P_{t}^{*}$ and Table 1 writes $p_{X}^{C}$ (capital C).

#### Structural shocks listed in 4.4.2 (printed p. 45, pdf_page 51)

The order follows the printed two-column list: the left column top to bottom, then the right column. Symbols are as printed in the list. Where the tables label the same item differently, the CSV column `enters_or_remarks` records it. "—" means nothing is printed in the list.

| # | Shock | Shock symbol | AR coefficient | Innovation | Specification / remarks as stated |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Transitory technology | $\varepsilon_{t}$ | $\rho$ | $\eta_{t}$ | AR(1) |
| 2 | Permanent labor-productivity | $g_{z,t}$ | $\rho_{g_{z}}$ | $\eta_{t}^{g_{z}}$ | AR(1) |
| 3 | Symmetric premium | $\varepsilon_{t}^{RP}$ | $\rho_{RP}$ | $\eta_{t}^{RP}$ | AR(1) |
| 4 | External premium | $\varepsilon_{t}^{RP^{*}}$ | $\rho_{RP^{*}}$ | $\eta_{t}^{RP^{*}}$ | AR(1) |
| 5 | Domestic premium | $\varepsilon_{t}^{DRP}$ | $\rho_{DRP}$ | $\eta_{t}^{DRP}$ | AR(1); no $\rho_{DRP}$ estimate is reported in Tables 2–4 |

Where the estimates are reported: AR coefficients for rows 1–4, 6–16 are in Table 2 (and Table 5); innovation S.D.s for rows 1–19 are in Table 3. For rows 20–25, Table 4 reports $\rho_{Y^{*}}$, $\rho_{\Pi^{*}}$, $\rho_{R^{*}}$ and the six S.D.s (Demand, Supply, Interest rate, World trade, Oil price, LR rate).

Cross-references (outside this chunk, for context only): the main-text equations give fixed coefficients 0.99 for the domestic premium (p.14, Section 3.1), the VAT rate (eq. 60), the inflation target (eq. 64) and the foreign forward real rate (eq. 90). This is consistent with no AR estimate being reported for these. In eq. (90) the innovation is printed $\varepsilon_{t}^{*,fwd}$, not $\eta^{*,fwd}$. The oil price follows an AR(2) process (eq. 93) with coefficients $c_{oil,-}$ and $c_{oil,\Delta}$ (Table 4), not an AR(1) with $\rho_{OIL}$; world trade follows eq. (91) with coefficients $c_{wt,\cdot}$.

### 4.4.3 Prior distributions

**Summary.** Table 2 lists the priors with the estimates. Footnote 30 notes that observation-equation parameters and shock S.D.s are in Table 3, and world-model parameters in Table 4.
- **Prior shape follows the parameter's admissible support:**
  - beta for parameters in (0,1): Calvo probabilities, AR coefficients, various weights;
  - gamma for parameters that must be positive, such as adjustment costs;
  - normal for unbounded parameters;
  - inverse gamma for shock standard errors.
- **Prior means** draw on estimates for similar foreign models and on pre-estimation calibration exercises. Those exercises judged model properties mainly through impulse responses, model-implied moments and historical shock decompositions. The main reference models are NAWM (euro area), RAMSES (euro area and Sweden), NEMO (Norway) and TOTEM (Canada). All are New Keynesian small-open-economy DSGE models with price and wage rigidities, incomplete exchange-rate pass-through, endogenous investment and an interest-rate policy instrument.

Specific prior choices:
- **Habit formation $\kappa$:** mean 0.7, mid-range of the reference models' estimates (0.57–0.88).
- **Indexation (all $\chi$):** mean 0.4. This lies between NAWM (0.5) and RAMSES (0.2) and is near Binyamini's (2007) estimate for Israeli prices.
- **Calvo probabilities (all $\xi$):** mean 0.6, i.e. a 2.5-quarter optimized price duration. This is somewhat shorter than the 3–4 quarters usual in macro studies, which the authors justify by Israel's relatively volatile inflation; it is also in line with Ribon (2004) and Binyamini (2007).
- **Adjustment costs ($\gamma_{I}$, $\omega_{\Gamma_{I}}$, $\gamma^{*}$):** taken from the pre-estimation calibration exercises.
- **External intermediation premium:**
  - $\gamma_{B}$ (on net foreign assets) follows the NAWM result. The text says 0.1, while Table 2 prints a prior mean of 0.01 (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T06.md)).
  - $\gamma_{S}$ (on the exchange rate) is 0.45, a little below RAMSES-Sweden's 0.6 and based on the calibration exercise.
- **Oil import share ($\omega^{*}$):** prior 0.15, roughly the fuel share in Israel's goods imports.
- **Policy rule:** standard literature values, close to other Israeli studies.
  - Smoothing $\phi_{R}$ 0.7, inflation response $\phi_{\Pi}$ 2.5, output-gap response $\phi_{y}$ 0.2.
  - These responses exceed Taylor's original (1.5, 0.125). Footnote 36: Taylor's 0.5 output coefficient refers to annualized rates, which is 0.125 for a quarterly rate. Because the rule includes smoothing, the short-run elasticities are actually smaller than the response parameters.
  - The depreciation response $\phi_{\Delta S}$ prior of 0.2 is not a standard literature value; it rests partly on earlier Israeli rule estimates (e.g. Argov and Elkayam 2010).
- **AR coefficients ($\rho$):** generally 0.7 (NAWM 0.75, RAMSES 0.85). The exception the text names is the four markup shocks, set to 0.3, reflecting the prior view that markup shocks (inflation-equation residuals) are unpredictable; Tables 3–4 show other exceptions ($\rho_{R^{*}}$ prior mean 0.25, $\rho_{EX}^{P_{OIL}^{*}}$ prior mean 0.50).
- **Prior standard deviations** express confidence in the means. Priors were kept wide in general, but tight enough to give a single posterior mode, i.e. enough curvature.

## 4.5 Estimation results

**Summary.** Tables 2–4 report the Bayesian estimates:
- Table 2: structural parameters and shock AR coefficients;
- Table 3: observation-equation parameters (section 4.2, Appendix B) and shock S.D.s;
- Table 4: the foreign-economy block (section 3.6).

In each table the middle panel gives the prior (shape, mean, std) and the right panel the posterior. Two methods supply the posterior figures:
- The **mode** comes from standard numerical optimization; its **std** is approximated from the inverse Hessian.
- The posterior **mean** and **5% / 95% percentiles** come from 4 Metropolis-Hastings chains of 700,000 draws, half discarded as burn-in. The scale is tuned for an acceptance rate of about 0.3, and convergence is monitored with Brooks and Gelman (1998).

**Identification (Figure 3).** Most, but not all, parameters are identified. Weakly identified ones, whose posterior largely reproduces the prior, are mainly the persistence parameters $\rho_{g_{z}}$, $\rho_{G}$, $\rho_{\nu^{*}}$, $\rho^{*}$ and the indexation parameters ($\chi$'s).

**Main findings.**
- **Nominal rigidity** is fairly low compared with other countries.
  - Imports are the most flexible: the estimate implies an average optimized price duration of 1.8 quarters, i.e. fast pass-through from the exchange rate and foreign prices to import prices.
  - Wage stickiness implies 2.2 quarters, below the prior (2.5) and below domestic prices (2.8). This may partly reflect volatile aggregate wage data rather than truly flexible wages.
- **Habit persistence** is consistent with the 0.7 prior.
- **Adjustment costs:**
  - Investment adjustment costs ($\gamma_{I}$) exceed the prior; the posterior mean is 3.3, still below NAWM (5.2) and RAMSES (8.7).
  - The data do not support an adjustment cost on the composition of world imports ($\gamma^{*}$); its posterior mean (0.65) is half the prior mean.
- **Modified UIP:** the data support the modification through $\gamma_{S}$ (see eq. (4)), but its posterior mean (0.36) is below the prior (0.45) and below the 0.61 posterior median for Sweden (Adolfson et al. 2008).
- **Policy rule:** mostly well identified.
  - Smoothing is strongly supported: mean $\phi_{R}$ 0.83, typical for extended Taylor rules.
  - The output-gap prior of 0.2 gets some support, with a posterior narrower than the prior.
  - The depreciation response is below the prior (mean 0.12 vs 0.2).
  - The data are uninformative about $\phi_{\Pi}$: the posterior roughly reproduces the prior (mean 2.5). The estimate is nonetheless broadly in line with other countries and earlier Israeli work.
- **Shock persistence:**
  - Technology shocks are relatively persistent: modes of about 0.9 for the transitory-technology ($\rho$) and investment-specific ($\rho_{I}$) AR coefficients. This suggests a sizable role for supply factors in Israel's cycle over the sample.
  - The four markup shocks have the lowest posterior modes (0.1–0.2). This mainly reflects their low priors, but the posteriors fall below the priors, so the data contribute too.
  - The external-premium persistence $\rho_{RP^{*}}$ (posterior mean 0.55, prior 0.7) agrees with Adolfson et al. (2008), who find that modifying UIP lowers this persistence; lower persistence means less predictable UIP deviations. Footnote 37: Adolfson et al. report a posterior median of 0.68 (prior mean 0.85) with modified UIP vs 0.93 without, and Christoffel et al. (2008) a mean of 0.88 (prior 0.75) with unmodified UIP.

Figure 3's prior curve for $\gamma_{B}$ (PDF p.60) is hump-shaped with its mode near 0.0073, which fits gamma(mean 0.01, std ≈ 0.005) and rules out mean 0.1. Gamma(0.01, 0.01) as printed would be an exponential with its mode at 0, so the printed std "0.01" is most likely 0.005 rounded. Do not use gamma(0.01, 0.01) as is (evidence from digitizing Figure 3 in the independent check).

Prior std "Inf" is printed as text and is not defined in the paper. All cells are kept exactly as printed.
- $\rho_{OB,\Delta Y}$ (normal prior) is the only negative estimate in this table: mode -0.185, mean -0.189, 5% -0.421, 95% 0.032.
- 14 observation-type S.D.s are listed (rows 1–14 of the S.D. block, Obs. error output def. to Exchange rate). This matches the "14 observation equation shocks" of 4.4.2.

## 4.6 Sensitivity analysis

**Summary.** This section re-estimates the model's posterior mode under five alternative versions of the interest-rate rule (62). The rule is chosen because it is the only ad hoc behavioral equation; the other behavioral equations come from agents' optimization. Each version is judged by how the parameter estimates change and by the marginal likelihood. Only the posterior mode is computed, because Metropolis-Hastings runs are very time-consuming.

The five alternatives:
- **Alternatives 1–2 (smoothing of rate changes):** add the lagged rate change $\Delta R_{t-1}$ with coefficient $\phi_{\Delta R}$, with a Gamma prior of mean 1.0. The motivation is optimal policy under commitment with a policymaker averse to rate volatility. Footnote 38 (Woodford 2003, Ch. 8.3): such rules contain $\Delta R_{t-1}$ with coefficient $\beta^{-1}$, slightly above one. Alternative 1 uses prior std 0.5; alternative 2 a tight prior with std 0.05.
- **Alternatives 3–4 (output growth):** add the lagged output growth rate (97.i7) with coefficient $\phi_{\Delta Y}$, inside the square brackets of (62), so its overall coefficient is $(1-\phi_{R})\phi_{\Delta Y}$ (97.i8). The prior is Gamma with mean 0.1 and std 0.05. Alternative 3 replaces the output-gap response ($\phi_{y}=0$); alternative 4 keeps both.
- **Alternative 5 (forward-looking):** the policy inflation measure (65), which mixes lagged and expected inflation, is replaced by the purely forward-looking measure 97.u2.

**Results (Table 5).** Estimates change little across specifications.
- **Alternatives 1–2:** the smoothing term affects the marginal likelihood.
  - A tight prior around one (alternative 2) worsens the fit (4429.7 vs 4444.6).
  - With the wider prior (alternative 1) the mode of $\phi_{\Delta R}$ is only 0.234, and the other parameters and the overall fit stay close to the baseline.
- **Alternative 3:** replacing the gap with growth does not raise the likelihood. The growth coefficient is 0.172, close to the gap coefficient, and smoothing rises slightly, probably because growth is more volatile than the gap.
- **Alternative 4:** including both gives a small gain (marginal likelihood 4444.6 → 4447.5). The gap coefficient stays about 0.2, the growth response is 0.15, and the other parameters are close to baseline.
- **Alternative 5:** the forward-looking rule barely changes the policy parameters but lowers the marginal likelihood somewhat. The authors find this at odds with the Bank of Israel's emphasis on (market-based) inflation expectations and with the good performance of single-equation Taylor rules using market expectations (fn. 39). They reconcile the two by noting that market expectations may differ from model-consistent ones.

Overall, the results are not very sensitive to the policy-rule specification; section 5 discusses the alternatives further.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i6; section: 4.6; page: 58; pdf_page: 64 -->
**(97.i6) Additional smoothing term in alternatives 1 and 2**

$$
\Delta R_{t-1}
$$

Meaning: The lagged change in the policy rate, added to the rule with coefficient $\phi_{\Delta R}$ (prior Gamma, mean 1.0).

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i7; section: 4.6; page: 59; pdf_page: 65 -->
**(97.i7) Lagged output growth term in alternatives 3 and 4 (coefficient $\phi_{\Delta Y}$)**

$$
\hat{y}_{t-1} - \hat{y}_{t-2} + \hat{g}_{z,t-1}
$$

Meaning: Lagged growth of output, i.e. the change in detrended output plus the lagged technology growth rate, entering the rule with coefficient $\phi_{\Delta Y}$.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i8; section: 4.6; page: 59; pdf_page: 65 -->
**(97.i8) Overall coefficient on output growth (term placed inside the brackets of (62))**

$$
\left(1-\phi_{R}\right) \phi_{\Delta Y}
$$

Meaning: Because the growth term sits inside the smoothed bracket of rule (62), its effective coefficient is scaled by one minus the smoothing parameter.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i9; section: 4.6; page: 59; pdf_page: 65 -->
**(97.i9) Output-gap response switched off in alternative 3**

$$
\phi_{y} = 0
$$

Meaning: In alternative 3 the response to output growth replaces the response to the output gap.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i10; section: 4.6; page: 59; pdf_page: 65 -->
**(97.i10) Coefficient on the lagged rate change under commitment (footnote 38)**

$$
\beta^{-1}
$$

Meaning: Under optimal commitment policy the coefficient on $\Delta R_{t-1}$ equals the inverse discount factor, slightly above one.

<!-- kind: display; id: 97.u2; section: 4.6; page: 59; pdf_page: 65 -->
**(97.u2) Inflation measure of the fully forward-looking rule (alternative 5), replacing (65)**

$$
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{CB} = E_{t}\left[\hat{\pi}_{C,t} + \hat{\pi}_{C,t+1} + \hat{\pi}_{C,t+2} + \hat{\pi}_{C,t+3}\right]
$$

Meaning: In alternative 5 the central bank responds to expected CPI inflation summed over the current and next three quarters, instead of the mix of lagged and expected inflation in (65).

Note: Equation (65), printed on PDF p.32 (outside this chunk), is $\hat{\pi}_{t}^{CB} = E_{t}\left[\hat{\pi}_{C,t-2} + \hat{\pi}_{C,t-1} + \hat{\pi}_{C,t} + \hat{\pi}_{C,t+1}\right]$, shown here only for comparison; it is not transcribed as a block of this chunk.

# Model evaluation and concluding remarks

> Part of the **MOISE agent edition**: an AI-readable restatement of Argov, Barnea, Binyamini, Borenstein, Elkayam & Rozenshtrom (2012), *MOISE: A DSGE Model for the Israeli Economy*, Bank of Israel Discussion Paper 2012.06. Not affiliated with or endorsed by the Bank of Israel. Equations, numbers and notation are transcribed from the paper; explanations are paraphrased. Conventions and verification status: [README](README.md).

Section 5 (moment fit, Table 6; in-sample forecast quality) and Section 7 (concluding remarks).

## 5 Model evaluation

*Printed pp. 62–68 (PDF pp. 68–74). The last paragraphs of 5.2 are on PDF p. 74, above the Section 6 heading.*

**Summary.** Section 5 tests the estimated model against the data in two ways. Section 5.1 checks whether moments simulated from the model bracket the observed ones: auto- and cross-correlations of six key variables (Figure 4), and means and standard deviations of eleven observables (Table 6). Section 5.2 compares the model's unconditional in-sample forecast errors with those of steady-state, random-walk and BVAR benchmarks (Figure 5).

### 5.1 Moment goodness-of-fit tests

**Summary.** Simulation gives the model's distribution of each sample moment, and the observed moment is compared with it. Most observed moments fall inside the model's 90% intervals; the exceptions are listed below.

How the test is built:

- Six key variables (inline block below): the policy rate, CPI inflation, the change in the nominal effective exchange rate, and growth in hourly nominal wages, output per head and exports per head.
- Data moments use the 72 quarterly observations of the estimation sample, 1992:Q1–2009:Q4.
- Model intervals come from 1,000 simulated samples of 72 periods each; for each moment, the band is the central 90% of its 1,000 simulated values. Parameters are fixed at their posterior means, so the bands reflect only randomness in the shocks, not parameter uncertainty (footnote 40). The simulated model includes its built-in filtering block (Section 4.2), so simulated series contain both the model's business-cycle dynamics and the imbalanced growth seen in the data.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i11; section: 5.1; page: 62; pdf_page: 68 -->
**(97.i11) Notation for the six key variables of the correlation tests**

$$
r_{t}^{OB}, \Delta P_{c,t}, \Delta S_{t}, \Delta W_{t}, \Delta Y_{t} \text{ and } \Delta X_{t}
$$

Meaning: Symbols for the six tested series: policy rate, CPI inflation, exchange-rate depreciation, and growth in hourly nominal wages, output per head and exports per head.

#### Figure 4: Cross-Correlations: Observed vs. Model-Based Confidence Intervals (page 63, pdf_page 69)

- **Layout.** The figure is printed sideways; it is described here with the page turned 90° clockwise, so that panel titles read upright and the caption runs down the right edge. It is a 6 × 6 grid of panels. Rows and columns both run over the six variables in the order $r^{OB}$, $\Delta P_{C}$, $\Delta S$, $\Delta W$, $\Delta Y$, $\Delta X$. The panel in row $a$, column $b$ shows the correlation of $a$ at $t$ with $b$ at $t-k$; panel titles read like "$r_{t}^{OB}$ & $\Delta P_{C,t-k}$". The diagonal panels are therefore auto-correlations. Panel titles use an upper-case $C$ in $\Delta P_{C}$; the Section 5.1 text and Table 6 use a lower-case $c$.
- **Axes and lines.** The x-axis is the lag $k = 0, \dots, 5$ (quarters) and the y-axis the correlation, from −1 to 1. The solid blue line is the correlation in the data; the gray band is the model-based 90% interval from the simulations above.
- **Auto-correlations (diagonal), approximate readings.**
  - $r^{OB}$: the data fall smoothly from 1 to about 0.3 at $k = 5$, inside a wide band (about −0.3 to 0.5 at $k = 5$).
  - $\Delta P_{C}$: about 0.3 at $k = 1$, near the lower edge of the band (about 0.25–0.55); near zero at $k = 2$–3; about 0.2 at $k = 4$, slightly above the band.
  - $\Delta X$: about 0.25 at $k = 1$, near the lower edge of a band of about 0.2–0.6, then declining toward zero inside the band.
  - $\Delta Y$: about 0.37 at $k = 1$ and 0.3 at $k = 2$, above the band (upper edges about 0.32 and 0.2). This is the output-growth persistence the model misses.
  - $\Delta S$: about 0.1 at $k = 1$, then near zero, inside a band that straddles zero.
  - $\Delta W$: the data zig-zag (about −0.25 at $k = 1$, +0.3 at $k = 3$, −0.3 at $k = 4$) and lie outside a zero-straddling band at those lags, only marginally at $k = 4$.
- **Cross-correlations (off-diagonal), approximate readings.**
  - $r_{t}^{OB}$ & $\Delta P_{C,t-k}$: the model band is positive (about 0.2–0.65 for $k = 1$–3), while the data are negative at every lag (about −0.12 to −0.25). The data therefore lie below the band at every lag, only marginally at $k = 5$. This is the main failure.
  - $\Delta P_{C,t}$ & $r_{t-k}^{OB}$: the data are negative: about −0.25 at $k = 0$, a trough near −0.5 at $k = 1$, about −0.27 at $k = 5$. The band also lies mostly below zero for $k \ge 1$ and contains the data from $k = 2$ onward; at $k = 0$–1 the data sit somewhat below it.
  - $\Delta P_{C,t}$ & $\Delta S_{t-k}$: about 0.35 at $k = 0$ and 0.2 at $k = 1$, then near zero, inside the band.
  - $\Delta Y_{t}$ & $\Delta X_{t-k}$ and $\Delta X_{t}$ & $\Delta Y_{t-k}$: about 0.56 at $k = 0$, inside a band of about 0.3–0.65, then decaying. At $k = 1$ (and at $k = 2$ for $\Delta Y_{t}$ & $\Delta X_{t-k}$) the data are at or slightly above the upper edge.
  - Other pairs: the data mostly stay within about ±0.3 and inside bands that straddle zero. Many panels, however, have one or two lags at or just past a band edge. The clearest cases are $\Delta Y_{t}$ & $\Delta S_{t-k}$ at $k = 1$ (data about −0.4, lower edge about −0.05), $\Delta X_{t}$ & $\Delta S_{t-k}$ at $k = 1$ (about −0.25 against about −0.1), $\Delta Y_{t}$ & $\Delta P_{C,t-k}$ at $k = 2$ (about +0.25 against about +0.08), and $\Delta W_{t}$ & $\Delta Y_{t-k}$ at $k = 2$ (about 0.42 against about 0.33). A few k = 0 bands do not straddle zero, so "inside bands that straddle zero" holds only mostly.

Findings stated in the text:

- The model implies significant first-order auto-correlation for $r^{OB}$, $\Delta P_{c}$ and $\Delta X$, and the data agree. The observed persistence of $\Delta Y$ is not captured. For $\Delta S$ and $\Delta W$, neither the model nor the data shows significant auto-correlation.
- Most observed cross-correlations lie inside the model intervals. The notable exception is the correlation of the policy rate with current and past inflation (inline block below): the model makes it significantly positive, but the data show no positive value. Sub-samples show none either. Footnote 41: two alternative observables brought no improvement either. One measured the policy rate as a gap to the long-run forward rate; the other kept housing in CPI inflation over the full sample.
- This moment depends mainly on the policy rule and its response to inflation. The rule is the model's only behavioral equation without micro-foundations, so the authors re-estimated the model under alternative rules. Some of these lowered the model correlation between $r_{t}^{OB}$ and lagged inflation but worsened other moments, so rule (62) was kept. Footnote 42 points to the sensitivity analysis in Section 4.6.
- In the reverse direction, current inflation against the lagged policy rate (second inline block below), the model reproduces the data's negative correlation fairly well. The authors read this as support for how the model transmits monetary policy to inflation.
- The correlation of inflation with current and lagged depreciation is also matched, which suggests that the model's exchange-rate pass-through to prices is reasonable.
- Many cross-correlations are not significantly different from zero in the model; Figure 4 indicates the same is true of the data.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i12; section: 5.1; page: 64; pdf_page: 70 -->
**(97.i12) Lagged inflation and the range of lags in the policy-rate/inflation moment**

$$
\Delta P_{c,t-k}, k \in \{0, ..., 5\}
$$

Meaning: Inflation lagged k quarters, k from 0 to 5; its correlation with the current policy rate is the moment the model fails to reproduce.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i13; section: 5.1; page: 64; pdf_page: 70 -->
**(97.i13) Lagged policy rate**

$$
r_{t-k}^{OB}
$$

Meaning: Policy rate lagged k quarters; current inflation's negative correlation with it, seen in the data, is matched fairly well by the model.
