Table note (paraphrased): the model intervals come from 1,000 simulated samples, each 72 periods long.

Findings stated in the text:

- Every observed mean lies well inside its model interval.
- Most observed standard deviations do too; where they do not, the observed value is below the interval. The text singles out output growth and hours-worked growth (inline block below). In the table, $\Delta C$ (1.22 against a 5% bound of 1.55) is also below its interval.
- The authors attribute part of the excess model volatility of $\Delta Y$ and $\Delta N$ to the exogenous law of motion for the change in inventories (equation (3)). In reality, inventory changes cushion output, which is demand-determined. Made exogenous, they instead add volatility, mainly to output and production. Footnote 43: dropping the inventory-change shock from the simulations shifts both standard-deviation intervals down and barely changes other moments, but the observed values stay below the new lower bounds.
- The interval for the mean of $r^{OB}$ is exceptionally wide (−2.15 to 12.89). This happens because the forward-real-rate shock (inline block below) follows a near-unit-root process (AR(1) with coefficient 0.99; Section 3.1, display 3.u1), which also shows up in the variance decomposition of Table 7 (Section 6). The authors note that this fits the trend in the observed interest rate over the sample. Footnote 44: without this shock, the interval narrows to 3.04–6.82, and other simulated moments change little.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i14; section: 5.1; page: 65; pdf_page: 71 -->
**(97.i14) Output and hours-worked growth**

$$
\Delta Y \text{ and } \Delta N
$$

Meaning: Growth rates of output and of hours worked, whose observed standard deviations fall below the model's simulated intervals.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i15; section: 5.1; page: 66; pdf_page: 72 -->
**(97.i15) Forward real interest rate shock**

$$
\varepsilon_{t}^{DRP}
$$

Meaning: Shock that moves the forward real rate; it is a near-unit-root AR(1) (coefficient 0.99), which makes the model interval for the mean policy rate very wide.

### 5.2 Forecast quality

**Summary.** The model's unconditional in-sample forecasts, up to eight quarters ahead, are compared with three simple benchmarks by root mean squared error. For most variables the model does no worse than the benchmarks, and it is the best forecaster of the policy rate. The authors expect conditioning on outside information to improve the forecasts in practice. They also name two shocks whose advance knowledge would sharply cut forecast errors.

How the comparison is built:

- Eight variables. For the Bank of Israel rate $r^{OB}$, the error is that of the rate forecast itself. For seven level variables (output, consumption, exports, the CPI, the nominal effective exchange rate, hourly nominal wages and hours worked), the RMSE is that of the cumulated growth-rate forecasts (inline block below).
- Benchmarks: steady state (SS; labelled "Model Based SS" in the figure legend), random walk (RW) and a third-order Bayesian VAR (BVAR). The BVAR uses Minnesota-style priors centred at 0.5; other prior means gave it larger RMSEs (footnote 45). It contains the eight variables of Figure 5. Adding employment growth (second inline block below) pushed the BVAR's RMSEs for $\Delta W$ and $\Delta C$ below those of MOISE, but raised them markedly for the other variables (footnote 46).

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i16; section: 5.2; page: 66; pdf_page: 72 -->
**(97.i16) Growth rates whose cumulated forecast errors are reported**

$$
\Delta P_{C}, \Delta Y, \Delta C, \Delta S, \Delta X, \Delta W \text{ and } \Delta N
$$

Meaning: Growth rates whose cumulated forecasts give level forecast errors for the CPI, output, consumption, exchange rate, exports, nominal hourly wage and hours worked.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i17; section: 5.2; page: 66; pdf_page: 72 -->
**(97.i17) Employment growth (footnote 46)**

$$
\Delta EM
$$

Meaning: Rate of change in employment; adding it to the BVAR benchmark lowered that model's errors for wages and consumption but raised them for the remaining variables.

#### Figure 5: In-Sample Forecast Root Mean Square Errors (RMSE) (page 67, pdf_page 73)

- **Layout.** The figure is printed sideways; it is described here with the page turned 90° clockwise (caption down the right edge), giving four rows of two panels (as printed, with the caption on top, it is two rows of four): "Forecast RMSE of $r^{OB}$" and "Cumulated Forecast RMSE of $\Delta P_{C}$"; then $\Delta Y$ and $\Delta C$; $\Delta S$ and $\Delta X$; $\Delta W$ and $\Delta N$ (all titled "Cumulated Forecast RMSE of …").
- **Axes and lines.** x-axis: forecast horizon, 1 to 8 quarters. y-axis: RMSE in %. There are four lines: DSGE (MOISE; solid black), Model Based SS (blue dashed with circles), RW (red dotted with asterisks) and BVAR(3) (magenta dashed). The legend is in the $r^{OB}$ panel.
- **Approximate readings.** Values are read off the chart at horizon 1 → horizon 8; they are not printed in the paper.

| Panel | DSGE | Model Based SS | RW | BVAR(3) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| $r^{OB}$ | ≈0.95 → ≈1.8 | ≈2.35 → ≈2.1 | ≈1.1 → ≈2.5 | ≈1.1 → ≈2.4 |
| $\Delta P_{C}$ | ≈0.85 → ≈2.5 | ≈0.8 → ≈3.2 | ≈1.1 → ≈7.0 | ≈0.9 → ≈5.2 |
| $\Delta Y$ | ≈1.2 → ≈3.9 | ≈1.0 → ≈4.1 | ≈1.25 → ≈9.3 | ≈1.1 → ≈5.0 |
| $\Delta C$ | ≈1.5 → ≈4.3 | ≈1.2 → ≈3.3 | ≈1.9 → ≈11.4 | ≈1.4 → ≈5.1 |
| $\Delta S$ | ≈3.2 → ≈7.3 | ≈3.3 → ≈9.5 | ≈4.8 → ≈28 | ≈3.7 → ≈16.8 |
| $\Delta X$ | ≈3.6 → ≈10.5 | ≈3.3 → ≈12.2 | ≈4.7 → ≈30 | ≈3.2 → ≈12.7 |
| $\Delta W$ | ≈1.8 → ≈4.2 | ≈1.7 → ≈4.5 | ≈2.7 → ≈15 | ≈1.7 → ≈6.5 |
| $\Delta N$ | ≈1.9 → ≈4.5 | ≈1.5 → ≈3.6 | ≈2.4 → ≈13.3 | ≈1.5 → ≈3.35 |

- **Main visible pattern.**
  - RW has by far the largest cumulated errors for every level variable, and they grow roughly linearly with the horizon.
  - $r^{OB}$: DSGE is lowest at every horizon. SS is nearly flat, and RW and BVAR almost coincide.
  - $\Delta P_{C}$: DSGE and SS are about equal up to horizon 4; after that DSGE is lowest.
  - $\Delta Y$: SS lies below DSGE up to about horizon 4 (e.g. ≈1.0 vs ≈1.3 at horizon 1) and the two are close afterwards. $\Delta W$: DSGE and SS nearly coincide. BVAR lies above both beyond the first few quarters.
  - $\Delta C$: SS is lowest throughout. DSGE is slightly above BVAR up to horizon 4; the two cross near horizon 5, after which DSGE is lower.
  - $\Delta S$: DSGE is lowest at all horizons.
  - $\Delta X$: DSGE, SS and BVAR stay close up to about horizon 5, with DSGE slightly above the other two at the shortest horizons; after that DSGE is lowest.
  - $\Delta N$: SS and BVAR are below DSGE at all horizons.

Findings stated in the text:

- For most variables the unconditional MOISE forecasts appear no worse than the benchmarks. For the interest rate, its RMSEs are below all the alternatives, which the authors take to mean the model predicts this variable better.
- According to the text, MOISE does not beat the SS benchmark for the CPI and output, unlike the interest rate. The authors relate this to flexible inflation targeting: it is chiefly the policy rate that departs from steady state, absorbing shocks so that other variables stay close to theirs.
- The authors call the result encouraging. In actual use, the forecasts are conditioned on much information from outside the model, which should improve them further (PDF p. 74). Footnote 47 adds that, according to Maih (2010), such conditioning can raise RMSEs, because models are inevitably misspecified. Conditioning is fairly easy in the DSGE model, hard in a BVAR and impossible with SS or RW.
- Two shocks would have improved the forecasts substantially had they been known in advance:
  - $\varepsilon_{t}^{RP^{*}}$, which hits the modified UIP condition (7); its standard deviation is large and its effects propagate strongly.
  - $\varepsilon_{t}^{RP}$, the very persistent risk-premium shock on bonds that enters the households' budget constraint (2).

  Forecasting with advance knowledge of these two shocks would cut the RMSEs considerably. Both symbols are given as inline blocks below; Section 3.1 defines them.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i18; section: 5.2; page: 68; pdf_page: 74 -->
**(97.i18) Shock in the modified UIP condition (7)**

$$
\varepsilon_{t}^{RP^{*}}
$$

Meaning: Shock in the modified UIP condition (7); large and strongly transmitted, so knowing it in advance would have reduced forecast errors substantially.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i19; section: 5.2; page: 68; pdf_page: 74 -->
**(97.i19) Bond risk-premium shock (budget constraint (2))**

$$
\varepsilon_{t}^{RP}
$$

Meaning: Very persistent bond risk-premium shock in the households' budget constraint (2); advance knowledge of it would also have cut forecast errors substantially.

Note: the paper prints $\varepsilon_{t}^{RP}$ here and calls it highly inertial, but elsewhere it uses that description for the domestic premium shock $\varepsilon^{DRP}$ (AR coefficient 0.99), while $\rho_{RP}$ is estimated at about 0.77 (Table 2). The sentence may refer to $\varepsilon^{DRP}$; transcribed as printed.

## 7 Concluding remarks

*Printed pp. 84–86 (PDF pp. 90–92).*

**Summary.** The conclusions restate what MOISE is and how it was estimated. They list its main modifications and their costs, highlight its distinctive properties, and outline further work.

- **Model and overall assessment.** MOISE is a micro-founded DSGE model of medium size, a type central banks use widely. It was built to help the Bank of Israel design monetary policy and assess risks. Standard Bayesian methods were applied to quarterly Israeli data, 1992–2009. The authors judge the outcome largely satisfactory: the data identify most parameters well, the simulated model matches most observed moments of key macro series, and its unconditional in-sample forecasts are on a par with naive and statistical benchmarks.
- **Modifications to the borrowed framework.** The changes, made to fit Israeli features and practical use, include among others (the paper's list ends with "etc."; Section 1 also mentions imports in government consumption and local-currency pricing of trade):
  - imports as an intermediate input to exports;
  - extensions for the time-varying long-term real rate;
  - allowance for the disinflation of the early sample years;
  - inventories, so that the aggregate resource constraint satisfies national-accounts identities;
  - stochastic terms in the observation equations that absorb imbalanced growth. This avoids pre-filtering and keeps the information in how the series move together.
- **Stated limitations.** Most modifications are very simple. They still improve the overall fit and the usefulness for policy, but simplicity has a price: some moments remain poorly matched. Neither the persistence nor the volatility of output growth is reproduced, partly because inventory investment is exogenous. Section 5 showed that output growth is more persistent and less volatile in the data than in the model. The model also fails to reproduce the data's negative correlation of the policy rate with past inflation, and the authors have no good explanation. An ad hoc, possibly misspecified policy rule is one suspect. Even so, the model forecasts the rate well, and the other structural estimates are robust to alternative rule specifications.
- **Distinctive properties.**
  - Different shocks drive real and nominal variables; the variance decomposition makes this duality plain.
  - In comparable models for other countries, monetary policy reaches inflation more through domestic prices than through import prices, with both channels pushing the same way. In MOISE the two channels are about equally strong.
  - Transmission is fast: after a policy shock, inflation responds most in the same quarter and output one quarter later. The effects also fade quickly by international standards, partly reflecting the weak real and nominal frictions estimated for Israel.
  - Prices are re-optimized on average every 1.8–2.8 quarters across sectors, against a typical 3–4 quarters elsewhere.
- **Planned extensions.** The authors hope to present applications and extensions of the model in future work. Beyond forecasting and risk assessment, the model already serves research questions, notably counterfactual and other simulations assessing how monetary policy was actually conducted. The authors are also considering richer labor and financial markets, as other central banks have done after the recent global economic events.

# Model properties: variance decomposition and impulse responses

> Part of the **MOISE agent edition**: an AI-readable restatement of Argov, Barnea, Binyamini, Borenstein, Elkayam & Rozenshtrom (2012), *MOISE: A DSGE Model for the Israeli Economy*, Bank of Israel Discussion Paper 2012.06. Not affiliated with or endorsed by the Bank of Israel. Equations, numbers and notation are transcribed from the paper; explanations are paraphrased. Conventions and verification status: [README](README.md).

Section 6: forecast-error variance decomposition (Table 7, also `data/table7_variance_decomposition.csv`) and impulse responses (Figures 6–10); Appendix E (Figures 11–18). The plotted impulse responses are digitized in `data/irf/`.

## 6 Model properties

page 68; pdf_page 74 (the heading follows the last paragraph of Section 5 on the same page).

**Summary.** Section 6 shows how the estimated model behaves. Subsection 6.1 reports how the forecast-error variance of ten key variables is divided among the 25 structural shocks at three horizons (Table 7). Subsection 6.2 presents Bayesian impulse responses to five central shocks (Figures 6–10) and explains their transmission. Responses to eight further shocks are in Appendix E (Figures 11–18). The section contains no numbered equations.

### 6.1 Variance decomposition

pages 68–72; pdf_pages 74–78 (Table 7 on pages 69–70, pdf_pages 75–76; footnote 48 on page 71).

**Summary.** Table 7 splits the forecast-error variance of ten variables among the 25 structural shocks at the 1-quarter, 4-quarter and infinite horizons; real variables are stationarized first. CPI inflation is driven mainly by the external risk premium shock and the price-markup shocks, with the oil-price shock gaining weight at longer horizons. Output is driven by demand-type shocks in the short run and by technology shocks in the long run (other real variables differ: see Table 7). The long-run variance of the policy rate is dominated by the nearly unit-root domestic risk premium shock.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 97.i20; section: 6.1; page: 68; pdf_page: 74 -->
**(97.i20) Deflator used to stationarize the real variables of Table 7**

$$
z_{t}
$$

Meaning: Before the decomposition, real variables are made stationary by dividing them by the labour-augmenting productivity level $z_{t}$ (introduced with equation 17).

**Main findings (own words; percentages from Table 7):**

- **CPI inflation ($\Delta P_{C}$).** Main contributors: the external premium shock, via the exchange rate (33–38% across the three horizons); the domestic and import markup shocks (14–21% and 16–23%); and, at 4 quarters and ∞, the oil price shock (8.7% and 11.5%). These shocks contribute little to output, its main components or hours worked.
- **Output ($\hat{y}$).** At one quarter, demand-type shocks lead: home bias 24%, change in inventories 22%, export share (demand for Israeli exports) 17%, symmetric premium (domestic demand) 11%. At the infinite horizon, technology dominates: transitory technology 36% and investment-specific technology 19%. The latter works both through investment demand and through the capital stock's effect on supply.
- **Imports ($\widehat{im}$).** Demand shocks dominate. At one quarter: home bias 33% (it changes the import intensity of final goods), inventory demand 18% (inventories are volatile and import-intensive), symmetric premium 14%, export share 13%. The symmetric premium shock moves domestic demand, part of which falls on imports, and it also moves import demand through domestic prices and, via the exchange rate, import prices. At the infinite horizon: symmetric premium 22.5%, foreign demand 18%, export share 17%. Higher export demand raises imports for two reasons: exports use imported inputs, and higher net exports appreciate the currency (through the external risk premium), which makes imports cheaper relative to domestic output. Footnote 48 adds that foreign demand also moves import demand through foreign prices, and hence import prices.
- **Real exchange rate ($\hat{s}$).** Its long-run variance reflects its two-way link with trade flows. Five shocks contribute more than 10% at the infinite horizon. Three of them are also the main drivers of exports and imports (symmetric premium, export share, foreign demand). The remaining two are transitory technology (19%; it works through domestic prices $p_{H,t}$ and the nominal exchange rate, see 6.2) and the external premium shock (13%; it moves the nominal exchange rate directly). At one quarter the external premium shock dominates (56%).
- **Labour market ($\hat{w}$, $\hat{N}$), infinite horizon.** Main sources: transitory technology (10.0% / 20.2%), symmetric premium (11.1% / 8.0%), export share (8.2% / 11.2%), foreign demand (10.2% / 9.0%) and wage markup (24.2% / 9.4%). All except the wage markup shift labour demand; the wage markup acts like a shift in labour supply. At one quarter the wage markup explains 66% of the real-wage variance, and transitory technology explains 38% of hours worked.
- **Nominal interest rate ($r^{OB}$).** At the infinite horizon, 75% of its variance is due to the domestic premium shock. This very persistent shock determines the long-run real rate, which varies over time. It dominates because it is assumed to be close to a unit root (AR coefficient 0.99); at short horizons its share is negligible. At one quarter the policy-rule deviation (interest-rate shock, 56%) and the external premium shock (26%, which moves the rate through the rule's reaction to inflation and depreciation) dominate.

### 6.2 Impulse response functions

pages 73–83; pdf_pages 79–89 (figure pages: 74, 76, 78, 80, 82 = pdf_pages 80, 82, 84, 86, 88; footnote 49 on page 73, footnote 50 on page 83).

**Summary.** Figures 6–10 show Bayesian impulse responses of twelve key variables to five shocks: monetary policy ($\eta^{R}$), external risk premium ($\eta^{RP^{*}}$), symmetric premium ($\eta^{RP}$), foreign demand ($\eta^{Y^{*}}$) and transitory technology ($\eta$). The text explains each transmission mechanism and compares the monetary-policy responses with similar estimated models. Footnote 49 refers to Appendix E for further shocks. Section 6.2 and Appendix E together show impulse responses for 13 of the 25 shocks; the paper shows none for the other 12 ($\eta^{g_{z}}$, $\eta^{DRP}$, $\eta^{C}$, $\eta^{G}$, $\eta^{X}$, $\eta^{FTR}$, $\eta^{\bar{\Pi}}$, $\eta^{\tau^{C}}$, $\eta^{\Pi^{*}}$, $\eta^{R^{*}}$, $\eta^{WT^{*}}$, $\eta^{*,fwd}$).

**How the responses are computed (own words, page 73):**

- The bands are Bayesian. They reflect uncertainty about both the shock's size (its standard deviation) and the model parameters.
- Size uncertainty: instead of a single fixed shock, shock sizes are drawn according to the posterior of each shock's standard deviation.
- Parameter uncertainty: the response distribution follows the posterior simulation draws of the parameters. A response is computed for every draw of the posterior simulation of Section 4.5.
- Each figure plots the mean response over draws, together with the 70% and 90% highest-probability intervals.
- Units: real variables in percent deviation from the model's steady state; inflation measures in percentage-point deviations; interest rates in annualized percentage-point deviations.

**Common format of Figures 6–18.** These points hold for every impulse-response figure in this chunk (6–10 here and 11–18 in Appendix E); each figure section below repeats the essentials. The visible caption is identical on all 13 figure pages.

- **Layout.** One boxed figure with 12 panels in 4 rows × 3 columns, in this order (titles as printed): row 1 Output, Consumption, Fixed investment; row 2 Exports, Imports, Hours worked; row 3 Interest rate, Real wage, Real exchange rate; row 4 CPI inflation, Domestic inflation, Imported inflation.
- **Shock size (caption).** A shock of one standard deviation.
- **Lines (caption wording).** Solid line: "mean of impulse response". Gray area: "70 and 90 percent highest interval of impulse response". There are two nested gray shades, a darker inner band and a lighter outer band. The caption does not say which is which; the nesting implies inner = 70% and outer = 90%. A thin horizontal line marks zero.
- **Units (paraphrasing the caption).** Real variables are in percent deviation from the steady state, inflation rates in percentage-point deviation, and the interest rate in annualized percentage points. The y-axes carry no unit label; each panel has its own y-range (listed below).
- **Horizon.** The x-axis has ticks 0, 10, 20 and no axis title; the text speaks of quarters. The mean line has vertices at x = 1, 2, …, 20 (checked in the PDF vector paths), so **x = 1 is the impact quarter** and 20 quarters are shown.
- **Sign convention (from the text).** A positive real exchange rate is a real depreciation. For example, the policy shock in Figure 6 appreciates the currency and the panel is negative; the external premium shock in Figure 7 depreciates it and the panel is positive.
- In the panel tables below, "Reading" describes the solid mean line. **All numbers in the readings are approximate** (rounded); exact values are left to the chart digitizer. "y-axis (printed)" gives the lowest and highest tick labels printed on the panel.

#### Figure 6: Impulse Response to an Interest Rate Shock

page 74; pdf_page 80. Discussed on pages 73–75 (pdf_pages 79–81).

- **Shock:** monetary policy shock, the one-standard-deviation innovation $\eta^{R}$ to the interest-rate rule (62); in Section 4.4.2 this shock is i.i.d.
- **Stated size:** one standard deviation (caption). The text says it raises the policy rate by about 0.75 percentage points at once.
- **Line types (caption wording):** solid line – "mean of impulse response"; gray area – "70 and 90 percent highest interval of impulse response" (darker inner shade and lighter outer shade; the caption does not say which is which).
- **Units:** as for all IRF figures (see "Units" under the common format in 6.2).
- **Horizon:** 20 quarters; the mean line is plotted at x = 1 (impact) … 20; x-axis ticks 0, 10, 20, no axis title.
- **Panels (in order):** Output, Consumption, Fixed investment, Exports, Imports, Hours worked, Interest rate, Real wage, Real exchange rate, CPI inflation, Domestic inflation, Imported inflation (4 rows × 3 columns).

**Transmission described in the text (own words):**

- Prices and wages are sticky, so the real rate rises too. Consumption and investment fall, for about two years.
- The higher rate appreciates the currency. Exporters, who have market power, gradually raise their foreign-currency prices, so exports fall, to about 0.2% below steady state.
- Imports face two opposite forces. Weaker domestic demand cuts demand for imported intermediate goods, while the appreciation shifts spending toward imports (expenditure switching). The switching force mostly dominates in the short run.
- Output falls by about 0.2%, because the demand contraction and expenditure switching both lower domestic activity. The trough comes after two quarters; output returns to trend in under two years.
- Inflation drops at once; summed over the first year the decline is about 0.4 pp. Two forces are at work: the appreciation lowers imported inflation directly, and marginal costs (wages and capital rental rates) fall. Marginal costs fall both because activity contracts and because the appreciation moderates wage demands. After weighting each component's response by its CPI weight, domestic and imported inflation contribute about equally to the fall in CPI inflation.
- Comparison with similar models (euro area: Christoffel et al. 2008 and Adolfson et al. 2007; Sweden: Adolfson et al. 2008; New Zealand: Beneš et al. 2009):
  1. MOISE's shock is 1.5–3 times larger, because the estimated standard deviation of its policy shock is larger.
  2. The output effect is smaller, mainly because investment is less sensitive. The inflation effect is larger, because exchange-rate pass-through is faster and import intensity is high.
  3. Output reacts faster and with less of a hump: its strongest effect comes after two quarters in MOISE, versus three to four quarters elsewhere.
  4. The effect lasts about two years, versus about five years elsewhere.

**Panels (mean line; all values approximate):**

|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Output | -0.3 to 0.1 | Falls; ≈ -0.18 on impact, trough ≈ -0.23 at x = 2; close to zero (≈ -0.01) from x ≈ 8 onward. |
| 2 | Consumption | -0.4 to 0 | Falls; trough ≈ -0.19 at x = 2; ≈ -0.05 by x = 6, then slow; ≈ -0.02 at x = 20. |
| 3 | Fixed investment | -0.4 to 0 | Largest fall on impact (≈ -0.20); uneven partial rebound (≈ -0.14 at x = 2, ≈ -0.16 at x = 3), then slow steady recovery; ≈ -0.03 at x = 20. |
| 4 | Exports | -0.4 to 0.4 | Falls; trough ≈ -0.23 at x = 3; crosses zero at x ≈ 8–9; small positive overshoot (≈ +0.02) around x ≈ 10–14; ≈ 0 at x = 20. |
| 5 | Imports | -0.2 to 0.2 | Small initial rise (peak ≈ +0.06 at x = 2), negative from x ≈ 4; trough ≈ -0.07 at x ≈ 6–8; ≈ -0.01 at x = 20. |
| 6 | Hours worked | -0.4 to 0.4 | Falls; trough ≈ -0.27 at x = 2; ≈ 0 by x ≈ 9. |
| 7 | Interest rate | -0.5 to 1 | Jumps to ≈ +0.78 on impact (text: 0.75 pp); ≈ +0.17 at x = 3; ≈ 0 from x ≈ 5 (tiny dip ≈ -0.02 at x ≈ 6). |
| 8 | Real wage | -0.05 to 0.15 | Rises ≈ +0.05 at x = 1–2; crosses zero at x ≈ 4–5; trough ≈ -0.02 at x ≈ 7–8; ≈ -0.01 at x = 20. |

| 10 | CPI inflation | -0.3 to 0.1 | Largest drop on impact (≈ -0.17); ≈ -0.07 at x = 3; ≈ 0 from x ≈ 5. Sum over x = 1–4 ≈ -0.41 (text: about 0.4 pp after one year). |

#### Figure 7: Impulse Response to an External Risk Premium Shock

page 76; pdf_page 82. Discussed on pages 75–77 (pdf_pages 81–83).

- **Shock:** external risk premium shock, innovation $\eta_{t}^{RP^{*}}$. The text notes that it is usually called an exchange-rate (UIP) shock (Section 3.1).
- **Stated size:** one standard deviation (caption). According to the text it causes an immediate nominal depreciation of 2.3% (the nominal exchange rate is not plotted) and a policy-rate rise of 0.5 pp on impact.
- **Line types (caption wording):** solid line – "mean of impulse response"; gray area – "70 and 90 percent highest interval of impulse response" (darker inner shade and lighter outer shade; the caption does not say which is which).
- **Units:** as for all IRF figures (see "Units" under the common format in 6.2).
- **Horizon:** 20 quarters; the mean line is plotted at x = 1 (impact) … 20; x-axis ticks 0, 10, 20, no axis title.
- **Panels (in order):** Output, Consumption, Fixed investment, Exports, Imports, Hours worked, Interest rate, Real wage, Real exchange rate, CPI inflation, Domestic inflation, Imported inflation (4 rows × 3 columns).

**Transmission described in the text (own words):**

- A higher external premium makes domestic-currency bonds less attractive than foreign-currency bonds. Equal expected returns are restored by an immediate nominal depreciation (2.3%) together with a higher domestic interest rate.
- The depreciation pushes up CPI inflation directly, via the cost of imported intermediate inputs; on impact the CPI responds to the depreciation with an elasticity of 0.18.
- The real depreciation improves the current account: exports rise (text: by roughly 0.5%) and imports fall. In other words, expenditure switching is stronger than the direct effect that the depreciation itself has on the current account.
- Domestic demand components fall, because of the higher real rate and dearer imports. Output nevertheless rises, by about 0.3%, because of expenditure switching.
- Higher inflation, higher output and the depreciation lead to a rate hike: +0.5 pp on impact, rising to 1.0 pp within three quarters (text).
- The depreciation is short-lived; the real exchange rate is back at its initial level within two years.
- Domestic inflation also rises, much less than imported inflation, because marginal cost rises. The text gives four reasons:
  1. Higher activity changes marginal productivity and input costs.
  2. The depreciation raises wage and rental demands: measured against the CPI (which contains import prices), wages and rental rates first drop.
  3. Lower investment creates a capital shortage, which raises the rental rate further.
  4. The higher nominal rate raises wage-bill costs through the working-capital channel (Section 3.2.1).

**Panels (mean line; all values approximate):**

|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Output | -0.2 to 0.6 | Expands; ≈ +0.22 on impact, peak ≈ +0.32 at x = 2 (text: about 0.3%); ≈ 0 at x = 5; mild undershoot ≈ -0.06 at x ≈ 7–8; ≈ 0 by x ≈ 18. |
| 2 | Consumption | -1 to 0.5 | Falls; trough ≈ -0.48 at x = 3; crosses zero at x ≈ 8; positive hump ≈ +0.15 at x ≈ 12–15; ≈ +0.11 at x = 20. |
| 3 | Fixed investment | -2 to 1 | Falls; trough ≈ -0.68 at x = 3; crosses zero at x ≈ 7–8; rises to ≈ +0.49 at x ≈ 15–17; ≈ +0.45 at x = 20. |
| 4 | Exports | -0.5 to 1.5 | Rises; ≈ +0.39 on impact, peak ≈ +0.79 at x = 3 (text: approximately 0.5%); crosses zero at x ≈ 7–8; undershoot ≈ -0.24 at x ≈ 13–15; ≈ -0.18 at x = 20. |
| 5 | Imports | -1.5 to 0.5 | Falls; trough ≈ -0.98 at x = 2; crosses zero at x ≈ 6–7; positive ≈ +0.28 at x ≈ 13–14; ≈ +0.21 at x = 20. |
| 6 | Hours worked | -0.2 to 0.6 | Rises; peak ≈ +0.37 at x = 2; crosses zero at x ≈ 5–6; small undershoot ≈ -0.04 at x = 7; ≈ -0.01 afterwards. |
| 7 | Interest rate | -0.5 to 1.5 | Rises to ≈ +0.51 on impact (text: 0.5 pp); peak ≈ +0.85 at x = 3 (text: up to 1.0 pp within three quarters); crosses zero at x ≈ 7–8; small undershoot ≈ -0.07 at x ≈ 10–13; ≈ -0.04 at x = 20. |
| 8 | Real wage | -0.6 to 0.2 | Falls; trough ≈ -0.41 at x = 2; crosses zero at x ≈ 6–7; positive plateau ≈ +0.09 over x ≈ 11–17; ≈ +0.07 at x = 20. |

| 11 | Domestic inflation | -0.1 to 0.3 | Rises; ≈ +0.10 on impact, peak ≈ +0.13 at x = 2; ≈ 0 by x ≈ 6; ≈ -0.01 over x ≈ 15–20. |
| 12 | Imported inflation | -0.5 to 1.5 | Jumps to ≈ +1.0 on impact; crosses zero between x = 2 and 3; trough ≈ -0.37 at x = 4; ≈ 0 by x ≈ 12. |

#### Figure 8: Impulse Response to a Symmetric Premium Shock

page 78; pdf_page 84. Discussed on pages 77–79 (pdf_pages 83–85).

- **Shock:** symmetric risk premium shock, innovation $\eta^{RP}$ (positive shock).
- **Stated size:** one standard deviation (caption). The text gives no impact size for the premium itself.
- **Line types (caption wording):** solid line – "mean of impulse response"; gray area – "70 and 90 percent highest interval of impulse response" (darker inner shade and lighter outer shade; the caption does not say which is which).
- **Units:** as for all IRF figures (see "Units" under the common format in 6.2).
- **Horizon:** 20 quarters; the mean line is plotted at x = 1 (impact) … 20; x-axis ticks 0, 10, 20, no axis title.
- **Panels (in order):** Output, Consumption, Fixed investment, Exports, Imports, Hours worked, Interest rate, Real wage, Real exchange rate, CPI inflation, Domestic inflation, Imported inflation (4 rows × 3 columns).

**Transmission described in the text (own words):**

- A positive shock makes risk-free financial assets (both domestic- and foreign-currency bonds) more attractive relative to real spending, so it acts as a domestic demand shock.
- Consumption and fixed investment fall on impact, by about 1% and 2%.
- Lower domestic final uses cut demand for domestic output (about -0.5%) and for imports (about -1.5%). Imports fall more because of expenditure switching.
- The resulting slack lowers domestic costs and inflation (by 0.2 pp over the first year).
- Facing lower inflation and output, the central bank cuts the policy rate gradually. The resulting depreciation causes expenditure switching, which explains why imports bear more of the contraction, and a gradual rise in exports of up to 0.7%.
- The effects last about ten quarters.

#### Figure 9: Impulse Response to a Foreign Demand Shock

page 80; pdf_page 86. Discussed on pages 79–81 (pdf_pages 85–87).

- **Shock:** foreign demand shock, innovation $\eta^{Y^{*}}$ (positive shock).
- **Stated size:** one standard deviation (caption). According to the text it raises foreign output by 0.6% and world trade by 2% (neither is plotted).
- **Line types (caption wording):** solid line – "mean of impulse response"; gray area – "70 and 90 percent highest interval of impulse response" (darker inner shade and lighter outer shade; the caption does not say which is which).
- **Units:** as for all IRF figures (see "Units" under the common format in 6.2).
- **Horizon:** 20 quarters; the mean line is plotted at x = 1 (impact) … 20; x-axis ticks 0, 10, 20, no axis title.
- **Panels (in order):** Output, Consumption, Fixed investment, Exports, Imports, Hours worked, Interest rate, Real wage, Real exchange rate, CPI inflation, Domestic inflation, Imported inflation (4 rows × 3 columns).

**Transmission described in the text (own words):**

- A positive shock moves the foreign IS curve (equation 89) up, so foreign output rises by 0.6%. The variance decomposition shows it is the foreign shock that matters most for the domestic real economy.
- There are three channels:
  1. World trade expands by 2% (equation 91), raising demand for exports; the text puts the export rise at roughly 2%.
  2. Higher foreign output creates foreign inflation (equation 92), which raises importers' marginal costs (equation 30).
  3. Foreign output and inflation trigger a foreign interest-rate rise of 0.5 pp within a year (equation 94).
- The initially wider gap between foreign and domestic rates pushes toward depreciation. Yet the real exchange rate appreciates, because the current-account surplus raises net foreign assets and lowers the external risk premium.
- Net effect: domestic output rises (peak about 0.4%), inflation rises (0.2 pp in the first year), and policy tightens (the rate goes up by about 0.3 pp within a year).
- The tightening first lowers consumption and investment. After about two years a positive income effect dominates and both rise above their steady-state levels.

#### Figure 10: Impulse Response to a Transitory Technology Shock

page 82; pdf_page 88. Discussed on pages 81–83 (pdf_pages 87–89).

- **Shock:** transitory technology shock: a temporary increase in total factor productivity $\varepsilon_{t}$ (equation 17), driven by the innovation $\eta$.
- **Stated size:** one standard deviation (caption). According to the text the shock peaks on impact and then decays, with persistence 0.86.
- **Line types (caption wording):** solid line – "mean of impulse response"; gray area – "70 and 90 percent highest interval of impulse response" (darker inner shade and lighter outer shade; the caption does not say which is which).
- **Units:** as for all IRF figures (see "Units" under the common format in 6.2).
- **Horizon:** 20 quarters; the mean line is plotted at x = 1 (impact) … 20; x-axis ticks 0, 10, 20, no axis title.
- **Panels (in order):** Output, Consumption, Fixed investment, Exports, Imports, Hours worked, Interest rate, Real wage, Real exchange rate, CPI inflation, Domestic inflation, Imported inflation (4 rows × 3 columns).

**Transmission described in the text (own words):**

- The text splits the response into a short run with excess supply and a medium run with excess demand.
- **Short run, supply side.** Given inputs can now produce more output. But with nominal frictions and a central bank that operates in the credit market, output is determined by demand. Demand rises, but by less than the direct supply effect. Because capital is predetermined, the capital rental cost and hours worked fall: technology replaces labour.
- **Short run, costs and inflation.** Marginal cost falls for three reasons: the direct productivity effect, the lower rental cost, and higher marginal labour productivity as hours fall. Together these outweigh the rise in the real wage, driven by households' income effect on how much they want to work and by sticky nominal wages. Lower marginal cost lowers DPI (domestic) inflation and then CPI inflation (by 0.3 pp in the first year).
- **Short run, exchange rate.** The result is a real depreciation, reinforced by a nominal depreciation as monetary policy eases; the peak real depreciation is 1.4%. Expenditure switching helps bring demand for domestic output in line with supply.
- **Short run, consumption and investment.** Consumption rises through the income effect and a lower real interest rate. Footnote 50: the real rate rises at first, because expected inflation falls by more than policy eases, but it declines from the second quarter on. Investment is forward-looking (adjustment costs, time to build capital). So although the real rate rises and the rental cost falls in the very short run, Tobin's Q rises immediately, reflecting expected lower real rates and higher rental costs later.
- **First few quarters overall.** Supply grows faster than domestic demand. This excess domestic supply shows up as higher exports and lower imports.
- **Medium run.** The shock fades while demand stays high, so production costs and DPI inflation rise. But the accumulated current-account surpluses cause a nominal appreciation that lowers imported inflation, so CPI inflation stays low for a while. Excess demand then raises imports, financed by the earlier surpluses.
- **Medium run (continued).** Market forces and policy together raise utility (higher consumption and leisure) long after the shock has faded. The current account cushions the gap between output's quick return and the long-lasting utility gain. Together with the relatively high persistence (0.86), these mechanisms keep the expansion going for more than five years in some variables.

**Panels (mean line; all values approximate):**

## Appendix E Bayesian impulse responses

pages 127–134; pdf_pages 133–140 (the appendix heading is on page 127 above Figure 11; References begin on page 135, pdf_page 141).

**Summary.** Appendix E has no text, only the heading and eight figures. Figures 11–18 show Bayesian impulse responses, in the same format as Figures 6–10, to eight further shocks that Section 6.2 does not discuss (footnote 49; 12 of the 25 shocks have no impulse responses anywhere in the paper): domestic price markup, import price markup, oil price, investment-specific technology, export share, home bias, wage markup, and inventory investment demand. Layout, caption, units, horizon and line types are as described under "Common format of Figures 6–18" in Section 6.2 and are repeated in each figure section. The shock symbols below are the innovation symbols of Table 7; the figures themselves print no symbols. The figures do not state the sign of the shock; the responses are consistent with a positive one-standard-deviation innovation.

| 7 | Interest rate | -0.1 to 0.1 | Rises ≈ +0.05 at x = 1–2; crosses zero at x ≈ 3–4; trough ≈ -0.055 at x ≈ 8–9; ≈ -0.036 at x = 20. |
| 8 | Real wage | 0 to 0.4 | Rises; peak ≈ +0.23 at x ≈ 3–4; slow decline; ≈ +0.09 at x = 20. |
| 9 | Real exchange rate | -1.5 to 0 | Appreciates; trough ≈ -0.69 at x = 4; slow return; ≈ -0.10 at x = 20. |
| 10 | CPI inflation | -0.06 to 0.02 | Small fall; trough ≈ -0.030 at x = 2; settles at ≈ -0.006 from x ≈ 10. |
| 11 | Domestic inflation | -0.05 to 0.15 | Rises to ≈ +0.09 on impact (maximum); crosses zero at x ≈ 3–4; trough ≈ -0.03 at x ≈ 6–7; ≈ -0.01 at x = 20. |
| 12 | Imported inflation | -0.4 to 0.4 | Falls to ≈ -0.22 on impact (minimum); ≈ 0 by x ≈ 5; small positive ≈ +0.03 over x ≈ 7–10; ≈ +0.01 at x = 20. |

<!-- ===== 09-steady-state.md ===== -->

# The steady state (Appendix C)

> Part of the **MOISE agent edition**: an AI-readable restatement of Argov, Barnea, Binyamini, Borenstein, Elkayam & Rozenshtrom (2012), *MOISE: A DSGE Model for the Israeli Economy*, Bank of Israel Discussion Paper 2012.06. Not affiliated with or endorsed by the Bank of Israel. Equations, numbers and notation are transcribed from the paper; explanations are paraphrased. Conventions and verification status: [README](README.md).

C.1 the non-linear steady-state model and C.2 the recursive steady-state solution. Equations (110)–(211).

## Appendix C The steady state

**Summary.** This appendix derives the deterministic steady state: C.1 restates the optimality and market-clearing conditions at the steady state (48 non-linear equations in 48 unknowns), and C.2 solves them recursively in terms of the parameters. Because productivity has a unit root and prices are non-stationary, variables with a real trend are divided by productivity $z_{t}$ and those with a nominal trend by the CPI $P_{C,t}$; lower-case letters denote the resulting stationary variables. Along the balanced growth path every real variable, world output included, grows at $g_{z}$, and every steady-state inflation rate is in line with the inflation target. Three features of the steady state are imposed: capital is fully utilized, net foreign assets are zero, and indexation makes the outcome equivalent to fully flexible prices and wages (all Calvo parameters set to zero).

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i2; section: C; page: 95; pdf_page: 101 -->
**(109.i2) Normalizer of real trending variables: productivity level**

$$
z_{t}
$$

Meaning: The productivity level, which divides variables with a real stochastic trend to make them stationary.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i3; section: C; page: 95; pdf_page: 101 -->
**(109.i3) Normalizer of nominal trending variables: the CPI**

$$
P_{C,t}
$$

Meaning: The consumer price index, which divides variables with a nominal trend to make them stationary.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i4; section: C; page: 95; pdf_page: 101 -->
**(109.i4) Example of a stationarized real variable**

$$
c_{t} \equiv C_{t}/z_{t}
$$

Meaning: Example of the lower-case convention: stationary consumption is aggregate consumption divided by productivity.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i5; section: C; page: 95; pdf_page: 101 -->
**(109.i5) Example of a stationarized price**

$$
p_{H,t} \equiv P_{H,t}/P_{C,t}
$$

Meaning: Example of the lower-case convention: the price of domestic intermediate goods measured relative to the CPI.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i6; section: C; page: 95; pdf_page: 101 -->
**(109.i6) Common steady-state growth rate**

$$
g_{z}
$$

Meaning: On the balanced growth path all real variables, including world output, grow at this one gross rate.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i7; section: C; page: 95; pdf_page: 101 -->
**(109.i7) Inflation target**

$$
\bar{\pi}
$$

Meaning: The inflation target. All steady-state inflation rates are consistent with it.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i8; section: C; page: 95; pdf_page: 101 -->
**(109.i8) Assumption 1: full capital utilization in steady state**

$$
u = 1
$$

Meaning: Assumed: capital is used at full capacity ($u = 1$) in the deterministic steady state; the cost of varying utilization is what delivers this.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i9; section: C; page: 96; pdf_page: 102 -->
**(109.i9) Assumption 2: zero steady-state net foreign assets**

$$
B^{*} = 0
$$

Meaning: The external risk premium, which depends on net foreign assets, pushes them to zero in steady state, so the current account is balanced.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 109.i10; section: C; page: 96; pdf_page: 102 -->
**(109.i10) Assumption 3: Calvo parameters set to zero**

$$
\xi's
$$

Meaning: Indexation updates all prices (and wages) by the target in steady state, so the solution matches flexible prices and all Calvo parameters ξ are set to zero.

## C.1 The non-linear steady-state model

**Summary.** The non-linear equilibrium conditions are evaluated at the deterministic steady state. Shocks are switched off (for most of them this means setting them to one), time subscripts are removed, and, since all households and all firms behave identically there, their indices are removed as well. The resulting conditions are listed by sector in C.1.1–C.1.9.

### C.1.1 Households

**Summary.** Steady-state household conditions: the consumption first-order condition gives the normalized marginal utility of income, and the two bond conditions tie the domestic and foreign nominal rates to growth, discounting and inflation (foreign inflation is assumed equal to domestic inflation). The capital accumulation law and the investment, capital and utilization conditions, with $u=1$ substituted, determine investment, Tobin's Q and the rental rate of capital. Full utilization is consistent with these conditions only under a restriction on the utilization-cost coefficient $\gamma_{u,1}$. Wage setting, once indexation is taken into account, makes the real wage a markup over the ratio of the marginal disutility of hours to the marginal utility of income, grossed up for household labor taxes.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 110; section: C.1.1; page: 96; pdf_page: 102 -->
**(110) Steady-state FOC with respect to consumption (marginal utility of income)**

$$
\lambda = \frac{\left(1 - \kappa/g_{z}\right)^{-1}}{1 + w_{\tau^{C}} \tau^{C}} \frac{1}{c}
\tag{110}
$$

Meaning: Normalized marginal utility of income equals the habit-adjusted inverse of consumption, divided by one plus the effective VAT rate.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 110.i1; section: C.1.1; page: 96; pdf_page: 102 -->
**(110.i1) Normalized marginal utility of income**

$$
\lambda_{t} = \left(\Lambda_{t} \cdot z_{t}\right)
$$

Meaning: The multiplier Λ_t times productivity z_t, a stationary version of the marginal utility of income.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 111; section: C.1.1; page: 96; pdf_page: 102 -->
**(111) Steady-state FOC with respect to domestic bonds**

$$
\frac{\beta}{g_{z} \Pi} R = 1
\tag{111}
$$

Meaning: Domestic bond Euler equation at the steady state. The discount factor times the gross nominal rate, divided by growth times inflation, equals one.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 112; section: C.1.1; page: 96; pdf_page: 102 -->
**(112) Steady-state FOC with respect to foreign-currency bonds**

$$
\frac{\beta}{g_{z} \Pi^{*}} R^{*} = 1
\tag{112}
$$

Meaning: Foreign-currency bond Euler equation at the steady state. It ties the foreign nominal rate to growth, discounting and foreign inflation.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 112.i1; section: C.1.1; page: 96; pdf_page: 102 -->
**(112.i1) Assumption: equal foreign and domestic steady-state inflation**

$$
\Pi^{*} = \Pi
$$

Meaning: Steady-state foreign inflation is assumed equal to domestic steady-state inflation.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 113; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(113) Steady-state capital accumulation (physical capital production function)**

$$
i = \left(1 - \frac{1-\delta}{g_{z}}\right) k
\tag{113}
$$

Meaning: Normalized investment covers depreciation and growth of the normalized capital stock.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 113.i1; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(113.i1) Definition of normalized capital**

$$
k_{t} \equiv K_{t+1}/z_{t}
$$

Meaning: Normalized capital is end-of-period capital K_{t+1} divided by current productivity z_t.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 114; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(114) Steady-state FOC with respect to investment**

$$
p_{I} = Q
\tag{114}
$$

Meaning: In steady state the relative price of investment goods equals Tobin's Q.

<!-- kind: display; id: 114.u1; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(114.u1) Steady-state FOC with respect to next-period capital (with u = 1)**

$$
Q = \frac{\left(1-\tau^{K}\right) r_{K} + \tau^{K} \delta p_{I}}{g_{z}/\beta - \left(1-\delta\right)}
$$

Meaning: With full utilization, Q equals the after-tax rental return plus the depreciation allowance, divided by the growth-adjusted real rate less undepreciated capital.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 115; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(115) Capital FOC combined with the investment FOC (114)**

$$
\left[\frac{\beta}{g_{z}} - \left(1 - \delta + \tau^{K} \delta\right)\right] p_{I} = \left(1 - \tau^{K}\right) r_{K}
\tag{115}
$$

Meaning: Substituting p_I = Q into the capital condition gives a link between the investment price and the after-tax rental rate of capital.

Note: Printed with β/g_z as the first term in the bracket. Combining 114.u1 with (114) gives g_z/β in that position, so this is a suspected typo (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T09.md)).

<!-- kind: display; id: 115.u1; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(115.u1) Steady-state FOC with respect to utilization (with u = 1)**

$$
r_{K} = \gamma_{u,1} p_{I}
$$

Meaning: At full utilization the real rental rate equals the slope of the utilization cost times the relative investment price.

<!-- kind: display; id: 115.u2; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(115.u2) Parameter restriction on the utilization-cost coefficient**

$$
\gamma_{u,1} = \left[\frac{\beta}{g_{z}} - \left(1 - \delta + \tau^{K} \delta\right)\right] \left(1 - \tau^{K}\right)^{-1}
$$

Meaning: The value the linear utilization-cost coefficient must take for 115.u1 and (115) to hold together at full utilization.

Note: Printed with β/g_z, like (115). Derived from 114.u1 it would read g_z/β, so this is a suspected typo (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T09.md)).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 116; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(116) Full capital utilization**

$$
u = 1
\tag{116}
$$

Meaning: Capital utilization equals one in steady state. The paper says this fails unless the restriction 115.u2 holds.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 117; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(117) Steady-state optimal wage setting**

$$
w = \frac{\varphi^{W}}{\left(1 - \tau^{N} - \tau^{W_{h}}\right)} \frac{N^{\zeta}}{\lambda}
\tag{117}
$$

Meaning: The real wage equals the wage markup times the marginal disutility of hours over the marginal utility of income, grossed up for household labor taxes.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 117.i1; section: C.1.1; page: 97; pdf_page: 103 -->
**(117.i1) Definition of the normalized real wage**

$$
w_{t} = \frac{W_{t}}{P_{C,t} z_{t}}
$$

Meaning: The stationary real wage is the nominal wage divided by the CPI and by productivity.

### C.1.2 Domestic intermediate goods firms

**Summary.** These are the steady-state conditions of domestic intermediate-goods producers. They cover the production function, the interest factor on working capital, the cost-minimizing ratio of capital to hours, and real marginal cost in consumer-price terms. With flexible steady-state prices, the relative domestic price is a fixed markup over marginal cost.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 118; section: C.1.2; page: 98; pdf_page: 104 -->
**(118) Steady-state production function of domestic intermediate goods**

$$
h^{s} = \left(g_{z}\right)^{-\alpha} \left(k^{s}\right)^{\alpha} N^{1-\alpha} - \psi
\tag{118}
$$

Meaning: Normalized supply of domestic intermediate goods is Cobb-Douglas in growth-deflated capital services and hours, minus the fixed cost.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 119; section: C.1.2; page: 98; pdf_page: 104 -->
**(119) Steady-state working-capital interest factor**

$$
R^{F} = 1 + \nu^{F} \left(R - 1\right)
\tag{119}
$$

Meaning: The gross financing cost of the wage bill is one plus the borrowed fraction times the net nominal interest rate.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 120; section: C.1.2; page: 98; pdf_page: 104 -->
**(120) Steady-state cost minimization (capital-labor ratio)**

$$
\frac{k^{s}}{N} = \frac{\alpha}{\left(1-\alpha\right)} g_{z} \frac{\left(1 + \tau^{W_{f}}\right) R^{F} w}{r_{K}}
\tag{120}
$$

Meaning: Capital services per hour rise with the effective labor cost (wage with payroll tax and working-capital financing) relative to the rental rate.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 121; section: C.1.2; page: 98; pdf_page: 104 -->
**(121) Steady-state real marginal cost of domestic intermediate firms (CPI terms)**

$$
mc = \frac{1}{\alpha^{\alpha} \left(1-\alpha\right)^{1-\alpha}} \left(r_{K}\right)^{\alpha} \left[\left(1 + \tau^{W_{f}}\right) R^{F} w\right]^{1-\alpha}
\tag{121}
$$

Meaning: Real marginal cost is a Cobb-Douglas combination of the rental rate and the effective cost of labor.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 122; section: C.1.2; page: 98; pdf_page: 104 -->
**(122) Steady-state optimal price setting of domestic intermediate firms**

$$
\frac{mc}{p_{H}} = \frac{1}{\varphi^{H}}
\tag{122}
$$

Meaning: The domestic intermediate-goods price is the markup times real marginal cost.

### C.1.3 Foreign intermediate goods firms

**Summary.** The importers' marginal cost is substituted into their price-setting condition. In steady state the relative import price becomes a constant markup over a cost that depends on the real exchange rate, the relative GDP deflator and the real oil price, raised to the oil share.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 123; section: C.1.3; page: 98; pdf_page: 104 -->
**(123) Steady-state optimal price setting of foreign intermediate (import) firms**

$$
\frac{s \cdot p_{Y} \left(p_{OIL}^{*}\right)^{\omega^{*}}}{p_{IM}} = \frac{1}{\varphi^{*}}
\tag{123}
$$

Meaning: Importers' marginal cost (real exchange rate times relative price times oil price to the oil share) over the import price equals the inverse markup.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 123.i1; section: C.1.3; page: 98; pdf_page: 104 -->
**(123.i1) Definition of the real exchange rate**

$$
s_{t} \equiv P_{Y,t}^{*} S_{t}/P_{Y,t}
$$

Meaning: The real exchange rate is the foreign intermediate-goods price converted into domestic currency, relative to the domestic GDP deflator.

### C.1.4 Domestic final goods firms

**Summary.** For each of the four final-goods sectors (consumption $C$, investment $I$, government $G$, exports $X$), the steady state gives CES demands for domestic and imported intermediate inputs, and the sector's CES price index. The CPI is the numeraire, so the consumption-sector price index is written as one equal to the CES aggregate, and the consumption-sector demands use $p_{H}$ and $p_{IM}$ directly.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 124; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(124) Demand for domestic intermediate goods: consumption sector**

$$
h^{C} = \nu_{C} \left(p_{H}\right)^{-\mu_{C}} q^{C}
\tag{124}
$$

Meaning: Consumption-goods producers buy domestic inputs in proportion to their output, scaled by home bias and the relative domestic price raised to minus the elasticity.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 125; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(125) Demand for domestic intermediate goods: investment sector**

$$
h^{I} = \nu_{I} \left(\frac{p_{H}}{p_{I}}\right)^{-\mu_{I}} q^{I}
\tag{125}
$$

Meaning: Investment-goods producers' demand for domestic inputs depends on their output and the domestic-input price relative to the investment-goods price.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 126; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(126) Demand for domestic intermediate goods: government-consumption sector**

$$
h^{G} = \nu_{G} \left(\frac{p_{H}}{p_{G}}\right)^{-\mu_{G}} q^{G}
\tag{126}
$$

Meaning: Government-goods producers' demand for domestic inputs depends on their output and the domestic-input price relative to the government-goods price.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 127; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(127) Demand for domestic intermediate goods: export sector**

$$
h^{X} = \nu_{X} \left(\frac{p_{H}}{p_{DX}}\right)^{-\mu_{X}} q^{X}
\tag{127}
$$

Meaning: Export-goods producers' demand for domestic inputs depends on their output and the domestic-input price relative to the domestic export-good price.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 128; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(128) Demand for imported intermediate goods: consumption sector**

$$
im^{C} = \left(1 - \nu_{C}\right) \left(p_{IM}\right)^{-\mu_{C}} q^{C}
\tag{128}
$$

Meaning: Consumption-goods producers buy imported inputs in proportion to output, scaled by the import weight and the relative import price raised to minus the elasticity.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 129; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(129) Demand for imported intermediate goods: investment sector**

$$
im^{I} = \left(1 - \nu_{I}\right) \left(\frac{p_{IM}}{p_{I}}\right)^{-\mu_{I}} q^{I}
\tag{129}
$$

Meaning: Investment-goods producers' demand for imported inputs depends on their output and the import price relative to the investment-goods price.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 130; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(130) Demand for imported intermediate goods: government-consumption sector**

$$
im^{G} = \left(1 - \nu_{G}\right) \left(\frac{p_{IM}}{p_{G}}\right)^{-\mu_{G}} q^{G}
\tag{130}
$$

Meaning: Government-goods producers' demand for imported inputs depends on their output and the import price relative to the government-goods price.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 131; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(131) Demand for imported intermediate goods: export sector**

$$
im^{X} = \left(1 - \nu_{X}\right) \left(\frac{p_{IM}}{p_{DX}}\right)^{-\mu_{G}} q^{X}
\tag{131}
$$

Meaning: Export-goods producers' demand for imported inputs depends on their output and the import price relative to the domestic export-good price.

Note: The exponent is printed as −μ_G. The X-sector analogues (127) and (135) use μ_X, so −μ_X would be expected. Suspected typo; C.2's (200) repeats it (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T09.md)).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 132; section: C.1.4; page: 99; pdf_page: 105 -->
**(132) Steady-state competitive price: consumption goods (CPI normalized to one)**

$$
1 = \left[\nu_{C} \left(p_{H}\right)^{1-\mu_{C}} + \left(1 - \nu_{C}\right) \left(p_{IM}\right)^{1-\mu_{C}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{C}}}
\tag{132}
$$

Meaning: The CES aggregate of relative domestic and import prices with consumption-sector weights equals one, the relative CPI.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 133; section: C.1.4; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(133) Steady-state competitive price: investment goods**

$$
p_{I} = \left[\nu_{I} \left(p_{H}\right)^{1-\mu_{I}} + \left(1 - \nu_{I}\right) \left(p_{IM}\right)^{1-\mu_{I}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{I}}}
\tag{133}
$$

Meaning: The relative investment-goods price is a CES aggregate of the domestic and imported input prices.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 134; section: C.1.4; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(134) Steady-state competitive price: government-consumption goods**

$$
p_{G} = \left[\nu_{G} \left(p_{H}\right)^{1-\mu_{G}} + \left(1 - \nu_{G}\right) \left(p_{IM}\right)^{1-\mu_{G}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{G}}}
\tag{134}
$$

Meaning: The relative government-goods price is a CES aggregate of the domestic and imported input prices.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 135; section: C.1.4; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(135) Steady-state competitive price: domestic export good**

$$
p_{DX} = \left[\nu_{X} \left(p_{H}\right)^{1-\mu_{X}} + \left(1 - \nu_{X}\right) \left(p_{IM}\right)^{1-\mu_{X}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{X}}}
\tag{135}
$$

Meaning: The relative price of the homogeneous domestic export good is a CES aggregate of the domestic and imported input prices.

### C.1.5 Exporters

**Summary.** The exporters' price-setting condition reduces, in steady state, to a fixed markup. The domestic-currency value of their foreign-currency selling price is a markup over the price of the homogeneous domestic export good that they buy.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 136; section: C.1.5; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(136) Steady-state optimal price setting of exporters**

$$
\frac{p_{DX}}{s p_{x} p_{Y}} = \frac{1}{\varphi^{X}}
\tag{136}
$$

Meaning: The domestic export-good price, relative to the domestic-currency value of the export price, equals the inverse of the export markup.

Note: The export price in the denominator is printed with a lowercase subscript, p_x. Elsewhere, e.g. (137), (142) and (152), it is p_X. Suspected typo (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T09.md)).

### C.1.6 Foreign retail firms

**Summary.** Foreign demand for the home country's exports in steady state. It depends on the export price relative to the price of world trade, on world trade scaled by foreign productivity, and on foreign technology relative to domestic technology.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 137; section: C.1.6; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(137) Steady-state foreign demand for exports**

$$
x = \nu^{*} \left(\frac{p_{X}}{p_{X}^{c,*}}\right)^{-\mu^{*}} wt^{*} \tilde{z}
\tag{137}
$$

Meaning: Exports equal a demand scale times the relative export price raised to minus the export price elasticity, times scaled world trade and relative technology.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 137.i1; section: C.1.6; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(137.i1) Definition of scaled world trade**

$$
wt_{t}^{*} = WT_{t}^{*}/z_{t}^{*}
$$

Meaning: Scaled world trade is the level of world trade divided by foreign productivity.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 137.i2; section: C.1.6; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(137.i2) Definition of the relative level of technology**

$$
\tilde{z}_{t} = z_{t}^{*}/z_{t}
$$

Meaning: Relative technology is the foreign productivity level divided by domestic productivity.

### C.1.7 Government

**Summary.** The government spending share of GDP and all tax rates are exogenous and treated as parameters. The government budget constraint is written in shares of nominal GDP, with inherited debt deflated by nominal growth. By the fiscal rule (61), lump-sum taxes respond only to the gap between debt and its steady-state level, so they are zero in steady state. Real government consumption follows from its share in GDP.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 137.i3; section: C.1.7; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(137.i3) Government expenditure share of GDP (treated as a parameter)**

$$
s_{G}
$$

Meaning: The share of government expenditure in GDP. The government sets it exogenously, so it is a parameter of the steady state.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 137.i4; section: C.1.7; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(137.i4) Tax rates (treated as parameters)**

$$
\tau^{C}, \tau^{D}, \tau^{K}, \tau^{N}, \tau^{W_{h}} \text{ and } \tau^{W_{f}}
$$

Meaning: The VAT, dividend, capital, labor-income and household and firm social-security tax rates are exogenous and treated as parameters.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 138; section: C.1.7; page: 100; pdf_page: 106 -->
**(138) Steady-state government budget constraint (shares of GDP)**

$$
\begin{aligned}
s_{G} + \left(\Pi g_{z}\right)^{-1} s_{B} ={}& w_{\tau^{C}} \tau^{C} \frac{c}{p_{Y} y} + \left(\tau^{N} + \tau^{W_{h}} + \tau^{W_{f}}\right) \frac{w N}{p_{Y} y} \\
&+ \frac{\tau^{K}}{g_{z}} \frac{r_{K} k}{p_{Y} y} - \frac{\tau^{K} \delta}{g_{z}} \frac{p_{I} k}{p_{Y} y} + \tau^{D} s_{D} + s_{T} + \frac{s_{B}}{R}
\end{aligned}
\tag{138}
$$

Meaning: Spending plus inherited debt (deflated by nominal growth) equals VAT, labor-tax and capital-tax revenue (net of the depreciation allowance), dividend tax, lump-sum taxes and new debt valued at 1/R.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 139; section: C.1.7; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(139) Steady-state lump-sum taxes**

$$
s_{T} = 0
\tag{139}
$$

Meaning: Zero in steady state: by rule (61) lump-sum taxes respond only to the gap between debt and its steady-state level, which is zero there.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 140; section: C.1.7; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(140) Real government consumption**

$$
g = \frac{p_{Y} y}{p_{G}} s_{G}
\tag{140}
$$

Meaning: Real government consumption is the spending share times nominal GDP, divided by the relative price of government goods.

### C.1.8 Net foreign assets and the current account

**Summary.** The trade balance, export and import shares of GDP are defined. The external intermediation premium drives steady-state net foreign assets to zero, so the current account balances. The trade-balance share therefore equals minus the exogenous foreign-transfer share.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 141; section: C.1.8; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(141) Trade balance as a share of GDP**

$$
s_{TB} = s_{X} - s_{IM}
\tag{141}
$$

Meaning: The trade-balance share of GDP is the export share minus the import share.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 142; section: C.1.8; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(142) Export share of GDP**

$$
s_{X} = \frac{s \cdot p_{X} x}{y}
\tag{142}
$$

Meaning: The export share is the real exchange rate times the relative export price times exports, divided by output.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 143; section: C.1.8; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(143) Import share of GDP**

$$
s_{IM} = \frac{p_{IM} im}{p_{Y} y}
\tag{143}
$$

Meaning: The import share is the value of imports at the relative import price, divided by nominal GDP.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 143.i1; section: C.1.8; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(143.i1) Zero steady-state net foreign assets (restated)**

$$
B^{*} = 0
$$

Meaning: Because of the external intermediation premium, the steady-state net foreign asset position is zero.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 144; section: C.1.8; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(144) Balanced current account in steady state**

$$
s_{TB} = -s_{FTR}
\tag{144}
$$

Meaning: With a balanced current account, the trade-balance share of GDP equals minus the share of foreign transfers.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 144.i1; section: C.1.8; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(144.i1) Foreign transfers share of GDP**

$$
s_{FTR}
$$

Meaning: The exogenous weight of foreign transfers in GDP.

### C.1.9 Market-clearing conditions

**Summary.** These are the steady-state market-clearing conditions: capital, domestic and imported intermediate goods, and the four final-goods markets (investment goods also cover inventory investment, export goods also cover the exporters' fixed cost). They are followed by nominal GDP, the aggregate nominal resource constraint (also at market prices, with VAT on consumption) and the definition of real output. The section ends with the profit shares of the export sector and of the whole economy. No adding-up equation for $h$ (the analogue of (147) for imports) is listed, because the paper states it is implied by the price, final-goods, import, GDP and resource equations.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 145; section: C.1.9; page: 101; pdf_page: 107 -->
**(145) Capital-market clearing**

$$
k = k^{s}
\tag{145}
$$

Meaning: Capital services used in production equal the normalized capital stock.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 146; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(146) Domestic intermediate goods market clearing**

$$
h^{s} = h
\tag{146}
$$

Meaning: Supply of domestic intermediate goods equals total demand for them.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 147; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(147) Imported intermediate goods market clearing**

$$
im = im^{C} + im^{I} + im^{G} + im^{X}
\tag{147}
$$

Meaning: Total imports equal the sum of imported inputs used by the four final-goods sectors.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 148; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(148) Final consumption goods market clearing**

$$
q^{C} = c
\tag{148}
$$

Meaning: Output of final consumption goods equals consumption.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 149; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(149) Final investment goods market clearing**

$$
q^{I} = i + \Delta inv \cdot y
\tag{149}
$$

Meaning: Output of investment goods covers fixed investment plus inventory investment, which is its GDP weight times output.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 149.i1; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(149.i1) Steady-state weight of inventory investment in GDP**

$$
\Delta inv
$$

Meaning: Inventory investment as a share of GDP in steady state, set outside the model (a given input).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 150; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(150) Final government-consumption goods market clearing**

$$
q^{G} = g
\tag{150}
$$

Meaning: Output of government-consumption goods equals real government consumption.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 151; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(151) Final export goods market clearing**

$$
q^{X} = x + \psi^{X}
\tag{151}
$$

Meaning: Output of the homogeneous export good covers exports plus the exporters' fixed cost.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 152; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(152) Steady-state nominal GDP (value added)**

$$
p_{Y} y = p_{H} h + s \cdot p_{X} p_{Y} x - p_{DX} q^{X}
\tag{152}
$$

Meaning: Nominal GDP is the value of domestic intermediate goods plus the domestic-currency value of exports, minus exporters' purchases of the homogeneous export good.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 153; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(153) Steady-state aggregate nominal resource constraint**

$$
p_{Y} y = c + p_{I} i + \Delta inv \cdot p_{I} y + p_{G} g + s \cdot p_{X} p_{Y} x - p_{IM} im
\tag{153}
$$

Meaning: Nominal GDP equals consumption, fixed and inventory investment, government consumption and exports, minus imports, all at relative prices.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 154; section: C.1.9; page: 102; pdf_page: 108 -->
**(154) Steady-state aggregate resource constraint at market prices**

$$
p_{Y}^{M} y = \left(1 + w_{\tau^{C}} \tau^{C}\right) c + p_{I} i + \Delta inv \cdot p_{I} y + p_{G} g + s \cdot p_{X} p_{Y} x - p_{IM} im
\tag{154}
$$

Meaning: The same resource constraint with consumption valued including VAT, which defines GDP at market prices.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 155; section: C.1.9; page: 103; pdf_page: 109 -->
**(155) Steady-state real output**

$$
y = h^{s}
\tag{155}
$$

Meaning: Real output equals the supply of domestic intermediate goods.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 156; section: C.1.9; page: 103; pdf_page: 109 -->
**(156) Steady-state share of profits in the export sector**

$$
s_{DX} = s \cdot p_{X} \frac{x}{y} - \frac{p_{DX} \left(x + \psi^{X}\right)}{p_{Y} y}
\tag{156}
$$

Meaning: Export-sector profits as a share of GDP are export revenue over output minus the cost of the export good used (exports plus fixed cost) over nominal GDP.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 157; section: C.1.9; page: 103; pdf_page: 109 -->
**(157) Steady-state total share of profits in the economy**

$$
s_{D} = 1 - \frac{mc}{p_{Y}} \left(\frac{h^{s}}{y} + \frac{\psi}{y}\right)
\tag{157}
$$

Meaning: The economy-wide profit share is one minus real marginal cost (relative to the GDP deflator) times intermediate output plus the fixed cost, both per unit of output.

## C.2 The recursive steady-state solution

**Summary.** The 48 steady-state conditions (110)–(157) are solved one unknown at a time (a recursive rather than fully explicit solution), so that the system stays easy to modify. Two choices start the recursion. First, the real exchange rate is normalized to $s=1$ and taken as given, and scaled world trade $wt^{*}$ becomes an unknown in its place. Second, steady-state dividends are set to zero ($s_{D}=s_{DX}=0$) and taken as given, and the fixed production costs $\psi$ and $\psi^{X}$ (of technologies (17) and (46)) become unknowns instead; the paper lists the 48 unknowns and the parameters that enter, and the next step, after these normalizations, imposes full capital utilization $u=1$ from (116).

<!-- kind: inline; id: 157.i1; section: C.2; page: 103; pdf_page: 109 -->
**Inline 157.i1: Normalization of the steady-state real exchange rate**

$$
s = 1
$$

Meaning: The steady-state real exchange rate is fixed at one and treated as a given input of the steady-state solution.

<!-- kind: inline; id: 157.i2; section: C.2; page: 103; pdf_page: 109 -->
**Inline 157.i2: Zero steady-state profit (dividend) shares**

$$
s_{D} = s_{DX} = 0
$$

Meaning: Both the economy-wide profit share and the export-sector profit share of GDP are set to zero and treated as given.

<!-- kind: display; id: 157.u1; section: C.2; page: 104; pdf_page: 110 -->
**Display 157.u1: The 48 steady-state unknowns of the recursive solution**

$$
\begin{aligned}
& wt^{*}, \psi, \psi^{X}, p_{Y}, p_{Y}^{M}, p_{X}, p_{I}, p_{G}, p_{DX}, p_{H}, p_{IM}, mc, r_{K}, w, R^{F}, N, u, k, k^{s}, \lambda, Q, y, c, i, g, x, \\
& q^{C}, q^{I}, q^{G}, q^{X}, h, h^{s}, h^{C}, h^{I}, h^{G}, h^{X}, im^{C}, im^{I}, im^{G}, im^{X}, s_{T}, s_{B}, s_{X}, s_{IM}, s_{TB}, p_{DX}, R, R^{*}
\end{aligned}
$$

Meaning: The steady-state variables determined by the recursion; world trade and the two fixed costs take the place of the real exchange rate and the profit shares.

Note: $p_{DX}$ is printed twice (first and second line) and total imports $im$, solved in (201), is not listed; transcribed as printed (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T10.md)).

<!-- kind: display; id: 157.u2; section: C.2; page: 104; pdf_page: 110 -->
**Display 157.u2: Parameters that affect the steady-state solution**

$$
\begin{aligned}
& s, s_{D}, s_{DX}, \alpha, \beta, \zeta, \delta, g_{z}, \kappa, \mu^{C}, \mu^{I}, \mu^{G}, \mu^{X}, \mu^{*}, \nu_{C}, \nu_{I}, \nu_{G}, \nu_{X}, \nu^{*}, \nu^{F}, p_{X}^{C}, \Pi, s_{G}, \\
& \tau^{C}, \tau^{D}, \tau^{K}, \tau^{N}, \tau^{W_{h}}, \tau^{W_{f}}, \varphi^{W}, \varphi^{*}, \varphi^{H}, \varphi^{X}, \tilde{z}, s_{FTR}, p_{OIL}^{*}, \omega^{*}, \Delta inv
\end{aligned}
$$

Meaning: The subset of parameters, including the normalized $s$, $s_{D}$ and $s_{DX}$, on which the steady-state values depend.

Note: the elasticities are printed with superscripts here ($\mu^{C}$, …) but with subscripts ($\mu_{C}$, …) in the equations; the competitors' export price is $p_{X}^{C}$ here but $p_{X}^{c,*}$ in (205); $w_{\tau^{C}}$, which enters 179.u1, (180), (207), (210) and (211), is not listed (see [transcription notes](transcription-notes/T10.md)).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 158; section: C.2; page: 104; pdf_page: 110 -->
**(158) Full capital utilization in steady state**

$$
u = 1
\tag{158}
$$

Meaning: The next step of the recursion, after the normalizations: steady-state capital utilization equals one, taken from the full-utilization assumption (116).

### C.2.1 Solving for prices

**Summary.** Relative prices are solved first, taking as given $s$, $s_{DX}$, $\varphi^{*}$, $\omega^{*}$, $\varphi^{H}$, $\varphi^{X}$, the oil price $p_{OIL}^{*}$ (calibrated at the end of this step) and the home-bias weights and elasticities $\nu_{C},\nu_{I},\nu_{G},\nu_{X}$, $\mu_{C},\mu_{I},\mu_{G},\mu_{X}$. Substituting (151) and (156) into nominal GDP (152) and using (155) links $p_{Y}$ to $p_{H}$; inserting this into the import-pricing condition (123) gives the import-to-domestic price ratio $p_{IM\_H}$ (159), and the consumption-good price index (132) then gives $p_{H}$ through the composite $A_{C}$ (160), hence $p_{Y}$ (161), $p_{IM}$ (162) and, via (122), $mc$ (163). The investment, government and export final-good price indices (133)–(135) give $p_{I}$ (164), $p_{G}$ (165) and $p_{DX}$ (166) through the composites $A_{I}$, $A_{G}$, $A_{X}$, and the exporters' pricing condition (136) gives the foreign-market export price (167). Finally, $p_{OIL}^{*}$ is calibrated so that $p_{IM\_H}=1$ (167.u1); with $s=1$ and $s_{DX}=0$ this gives $p_{Y}=p_{I}=p_{G}=p_{H}=p_{IM}=p_{DX}=1$, $mc=1/\varphi^{H}$ and $p_{X}=\varphi^{X}$, so the steady state does not depend on the substitution elasticities.

<!-- kind: display; id: 158.u1; section: C.2.1; page: 104; pdf_page: 110 -->
**Display 158.u1: Nominal GDP with the export profit share substituted**

$$
p_{Y} y = p_{H} h + p_{Y} y s_{DX}
$$

Meaning: Nominal GDP equals the value of domestic intermediate output plus export-sector profits, the latter written as their share of nominal GDP.

<!-- kind: display; id: 158.u2; section: C.2.1; page: 104; pdf_page: 110 -->
**Display 158.u2: Output deflator in terms of the domestic intermediate price**

$$
p_{Y} = \left(1 - s_{DX}\right)^{-1} p_{H}
$$

Meaning: The output deflator equals the domestic intermediate-goods price scaled up by one over one minus the export profit share.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 159; section: C.2.1; page: 104; pdf_page: 110 -->
**(159) Ratio of the import price to the domestic intermediate price**

$$
p_{IM\_H} \left(= \frac{p_{IM}}{p_{H}}\right) = \varphi^{*} \left(p_{OIL}^{*}\right)^{\omega^{*}} s \left(1 - s_{DX}\right)^{-1}
\tag{159}
$$

Meaning: Defines $p_{IM\_H}$ as $p_{IM}/p_{H}$ and expresses it through the import markup, the oil price raised to the oil share, the real exchange rate and the export profit share.

<!-- kind: display; id: 159.u1; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**Display 159.u1: Consumption-good price index (132) rewritten to solve for $p_{H}$**

$$
\frac{1}{p_{H}} = \left[\nu_{C} + \left(1 - \nu_{C}\right) \left(\frac{p_{IM}}{p_{H}}\right)^{1-\mu_{C}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{C}}}
$$

Meaning: With the consumption good as numeraire, the inverse domestic intermediate price is a CES aggregate of one and the import-to-domestic price ratio.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 160; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**(160) Domestic intermediate-goods price and the composite $A_{C}$**

$$
p_{H} = \left(A_{C}\right)^{-1} \quad \text{with} \quad A_{C} = \left[\nu_{C} + \left(1 - \nu_{C}\right) \left(p_{IM\_H}\right)^{1-\mu_{C}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{C}}}
\tag{160}
$$

Meaning: The relative domestic intermediate price is the inverse of a consumption-sector CES composite of one and the import-to-domestic price ratio.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 161; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**(161) Output deflator**

$$
p_{Y} = \left(1 - s_{DX}\right)^{-1} \left(A_{C}\right)^{-1}
\tag{161}
$$

Meaning: The output deflator is the domestic intermediate price from (160) scaled by the export profit-share term.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 162; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**(162) Price of imported intermediate goods**

$$
p_{IM} = \varphi^{*} \left(p_{OIL}^{*}\right)^{\omega^{*}} s \cdot \left(1 - s_{DX}\right)^{-1} \left(A_{C}\right)^{-1}
\tag{162}
$$

Meaning: The relative import price equals the import-to-domestic price ratio (159) times the domestic intermediate price (160).

<!-- kind: eq; id: 163; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**(163) Real marginal cost of domestic intermediate-goods firms**

$$
mc = \frac{1}{\varphi^{H}} \left(A_{C}\right)^{-1}
\tag{163}
$$

Meaning: Domestic firms' real marginal cost equals the domestic intermediate price divided by the domestic price markup.

<!-- kind: display; id: 163.u1; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**Display 163.u1: Investment-good price index (133) with $p_{H}$ factored out**

$$
p_{I} = p_{H} \left[\nu_{I} + \left(1 - \nu_{I}\right) \left(\frac{p_{IM}}{p_{H}}\right)^{1-\mu_{I}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{I}}}
$$

Meaning: The investment-good price is the domestic intermediate price times a CES aggregate of one and the import-to-domestic price ratio.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 164; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**(164) Investment-good price and the composite $A_{I}$**

$$
p_{I} = \left(A_{C}\right)^{-1} A_{I} \quad \text{with} \quad A_{I} = \left[\nu_{I} + \left(1 - \nu_{I}\right) \left(p_{IM\_H}\right)^{1-\mu_{I}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{I}}}
\tag{164}
$$

Meaning: The relative investment-good price equals the investment-sector CES composite of the price ratio divided by the consumption composite $A_{C}$.

<!-- kind: display; id: 164.u1; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**Display 164.u1: Government-consumption-good price index (134) with $p_{H}$ factored out**

$$
p_{G} = p_{H} \left[\nu_{G} + \left(1 - \nu_{G}\right) \left(\frac{p_{IM}}{p_{H}}\right)^{1-\mu_{G}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{G}}}
$$

Meaning: The government-good price is the domestic intermediate price times a CES aggregate of one and the import-to-domestic price ratio.

<!-- kind: eq; id: 165; section: C.2.1; page: 105; pdf_page: 111 -->
**(165) Government-consumption-good price and the composite $A_{G}$**

$$
p_{G} = \left(A_{C}\right)^{-1} A_{G} \quad \text{with} \quad A_{G} = \left[\nu_{G} + \left(1 - \nu_{G}\right) \left(p_{IM\_H}\right)^{1-\mu_{G}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1-\mu_{G}}}
\tag{165}
$$
